(18; original 312): Caesar of Heisterbach, Dial., X. 22, speaks of a woman suffering with severe pains in childbirth who was delivered with ease, so soon as she consented to her husband’s going on a crusade.

(19; original 313): The name Franks became the current designation for Europeans in the East, and remains so to this day. The crusading enthusiasm did not fully take hold of Germany till the twelfth century. Hauck, Kirchengesch. Deutschlands, IV. 80.

(20; original 314): The expression was a translation of the Latin ultra mare, used for the East, and, so far as I know, for the first time by Gregory VII., Reg. II. 37; Migne, 148, 390.

(21; original 315): Gregorovius, IV. 288, says no traces of enthusiasm can be found in Rome. "Senate and people would probably have laughed in derision had Urban summoned them to rise in religious enthusiasm to forsake the ruins of Rome and advance to the rescue of Jerusalem." The Crusades were a financial detriment to Rome by diverting pilgrimages from the tombs of the Apostles to the tomb of the Saviour.

(22; original 316): Here is one such miracle. At the battle of Ramleh, 1177, there was a miraculous extension of the cross borne by the bishop of Bethlehem. It reached to heaven and extended its arms across the whole horizon. The pagans saw it, were confused, and fled. Hoveden, II. 133 sq.

(23; original 317): Hegel, Philosophie der Gesch., 3d ed. 1848, p. 476, brings out this idea most impressively.

(24; original 318): Röhricht, Gesch. d. ersten Kreuzzuges, p. 6, says that in these struggles "the crusading enthusiasm was born."

(25; original 319): See the beautiful testimony of Gregory, who advised a Cappadocian abbot against going with his monks to Jerusalem, Schaff, Ch. Hist. III. 906.

(26; original 320): Fulke the Black, count of Anjou (987-1040), made three journeys to Jerusalem in penance for sacrilege and other crimes. He had burned his young wife at the stake dressed in her gayest attire, and caused his son to crouch at his feet harnessed as an ass. At Jerusalem he showed his devotion by going about with a halter about his neck. He bit off a piece of the Lord’s tombstone with his teeth and carried back to Europe objects most sacred and priceless, such as the fingers of Apostles and the lamp in which the holy fire was lit. Odolric, bishop of Orleans, gave a pound of gold for the lamp and hung it up in the church at Orleans, where its virtue cured multitudes of sick people.

(27; original 321): Hauck, IV. 79.

(28; original 322): Ekkehard, 5, Rec., V. 14, may exaggerate when he speaks of very frequent letters and embassies from the Greek emperors to the West, per legationes frequentissimas et epistolas etiam a nobis visas ... lugubriter inclamanter, etc. The letter of Alexius to Robert of Flanders, 1088, has been the subject of much inquiry. Hagenmeyer pronounces it genuine, after a most careful investigation, Epistulae, etc., 10-44.

(29; original 323): Diehl, in Essays on the Crusades, 92, seems even to deny that an appeal was ever made by the Byzantine emperor Alexius for aid to the West, and speaks of it as an invention of a later time. Certainly no criticism could be more unwarranted unless all the testimonies of the contemporary writers are to be ruthlessly set aside.

(30; original 324): Reg., I. 49; II. 37, Migne, 148, 329, 390.

(31; original 325): multa millia Christianorum quasi pecudes occidisse, Reg., I. 49

(32; original 326): See Jules Lair, Études crit. sur divers textes des Xeet XIesiècles. Bulle du pape Sergius IV., etc., Paris, 1899. Lair, in opposition to Riant, Pflugk-Harttung, etc., gives reasons for accepting as genuine Sergius’s letter, found 1857. For Sylvester’s letter see Havet, Lettres de Gerbert, Paris, 1889. Röhricht, Gesch. d. ersten Kreuzzuges, 8, pronounces Sylvester’s letter a forgery, dating from 1095. Lair tries to prove it was written by Sergius IV.

(33; original 327): The date of the pilgrimage is not given, but may be accepted as having fallen between 1092-1094. Peter is called "the Hermit" by all the accounts, begining with the earliest, the Gesta Francorum. There is no good ground for doubting that he was from Amiens, as Albert of Aachen distinctly states. William of Tyre says from the "bishopric of Amiens." Hagenmeyer, p. 39, accepts the latter as within the truth.

(34; original 328): William of Tyre, Bk. I. 12, Rec., I. 35, gives only a few lines to the visions and the words spoken by the Lord. His account of the meeting with Urban is equally simple and scarcely less brief. Peter found, so he writes, "the Lord Pope Urban in the vicinity of Rome and presented the letters from the patriarch and Christians of Jerusalem and showed their misery and the abominations which the unclean races wrought in the holy places. Thus prudently and faithfully he performed the commission intrusted to him."

(35; original 329): At the Council of Clermont Urban made reference to the "very many reports" which had come of the desolation of Jerusalem, Fulcher, Rec., III. 324. Robert the Monk, I. 1, Rec., III. 727, says relatio gravis saepissime jam ad aures nostras pervenit. According to Baldric he appealed to the many among his hearers who could vouch for the desolate condition of the holy places from their own experience, Rec., IV. 14. See Hagenmeyer, 74-77.

(36; original 330): So William of Tyre, Bk. I. 13. Later writers extend the journey of Peter inordinately.

(37; original 331): William of Tyre does not mention this embassy. It may be because of the low opinion he had of Alexius, whom (II. 5) he pronounces scheming and perfidious.

(38; original 332): There is no statement that the council formally decreed the Crusade. For the acts we are dependent upon scattered statements of chroniclers and several other unofficial documents.

(39; original 333): Ranke, Weltgeschichte. According to William of Tyre, Peter the Hermit was present at Clermont. The contemporary writers do not mention his presence.

(40; original 334): Gregorovius, IV. 287, is right when he says, "the Importance of Urban’s speech in universal history outweighs the orations of Demosthenes and Cicero."

(41; original 335): Robert the Monk, I. 1, Rec., III. 727. The contemporary writers, giving an account of Urban’s speech, are Baldric, Guibert, Fulcher, and Robert the Monk. All of them were present at Clermont. William of Tyre greatly elaborates the address, and Röhricht calls William’s account an invention which is a masterpiece of its kind,—eine Erdichtung die ein Meisterstück seiner Art, etc., Gesch. des ersten Kreuzzuges, p. 20. Röhricht, pp. 235-239, and Munro, "Am. Hist. Rev.," 1906, pp. 231-243, make interesting attempts to reconstruct Urban’s address. The different accounts are not to be regarded as contradictory, but as supplementary one of the other. Röhricht, p. 20, expresses the opinion that none of the accounts of the address is "accurate." No doubt the spirit and essential contents are preserved. Urban made prominent the appeals for aid from the East, the desolations of Jerusalem, and the sufferings of Christians in the East. See Munro.

(42; original 336): Fulcher, Rec., III. 324. I follow chiefly the accounts of Fulcher and Robert. Robert represents the appeals for aid as coming from Jerusalem and Constantinople.

(43; original 337): Robert the Monk, I. 2 Rec., III. 729. The expression "navel of the earth,"umbilicus terrarum, used by Robert, was a common one for Jerusalem.

(44; original 338): Baldric, Rec., IV. 15, via brevis est, labor permodicus est qui tamen immarcescibilem vobis rependet coronam. Gregory VII., Reg., II. 37, Migne, 148, 390, had made the same promise, quoting 2 Cor. iv. 17, that for the toils of a moment the Crusaders would secure an eternal reward.

(45; original 339): Deus vult, Deos lo volt, Diex el volt. These are the different forms in which the response is reported. For this response in its Latin form, Robert the Monk is our earliest authority, I. 2, Rec., III. 729. He says una vociferatio "Deus vult, Deus vult."

(46; original 340): In the First Crusade all the crosses were red. Afterwards green and white colors came into use. Urban himself distributed crosses. Guibert, II. 5, Rec., IV. 140, and Fulcher, I. 4, state that Urban had the Crusaders wear the cross as a badge.

(47; original 341): Urban’s letters, following up his speech at Clermont, are given by Hagenmeyer, Epistulae, p. 136 sqq.

(48; original 342): Petrum more heremi vilissima cappa tegebat, Radulf of Caen. The above description is taken from strictly contemporary accounts.

(49; original 343): The statura brevis of Radulf becomes in William of Tyre’s account pusillus, persona contemptibilis.

(50; original 344): I have thus translated Radulf’s spiritus acer.

(51; original 345): Albert of Aachen: neminem invenerunt qui tam ferocissimo et superbo loqui auderet quousque Petrus.

(52; original 346): So Guibert speaks of the crowds listening to him as tanta populorum multitudo. Hagenmeyer, p. 114, accepting Guibert’s statement, refers to immense throngs, ungeheure Zahl.

(53; original 347): Guibert: quidquid agebat namque seu loquebatur quasi quiddam subdivinum videbatur.

(54; original 348): So Ekkehard, XII., Rec., V. 20 sq. who has something derogatory to say of all of these preachers and also of Peter’s subsequent career. Quem postea multi hypocritam esse dicebant.

(55; original 349): Robert the Monk, I. 5, Rec., III. 731. Super ipsos praesules et abbates apice religionis efferebatur.

(56; original 350): Guibert: neminem meminerim similem honore haberi. Baldric speaks of him as Petrus quidam magnus heremita, or as we would say, "that great hermit, Peter."

(57; original 351): Hegel, Philosophie der Gesch., p. 444, calls the Crusades "the culminating point of the Middle Ages." Contemporaries like Guibert of Nogent, 123, could think of no movement equal in glory with the Crusades. Ordericus Vitalis, III. 458, praised the union of peoples of different tongues in a project so praiseworthy.

(58; original 352): For the account of these early expeditions, we are chiefly dependent upon Albert of Aachen. Guibert makes no distinction of sections, and has only a cursory notice of the expeditions before the arrival of Peter in Constantinople.

(59; original 353): Sine Pecunia, Sansavoir, Habenichts. These preliminary expeditions, Röhricht and other historians call Die Züge der Bauern, the campaigns of the peasants.

(60; original 354): See Hagenmeyer, 204 sq. Peter apologized to the emperor for the defeat on the ground of his inability to control his followers, who, he declared, were unworthy to see Jerusalem. Anna Comnena calls Peter the "inflated Latin."

(61; original 355): I. 26.

(62; original 356): Anna Comnena says the Crusaders flowed together from all directions like rivers. She gives the number of Peter’s army as eighty thousand foot and one hundred thousand horse. Fulcher speaks of the numbers setting out from the West as "an immense assemblage. The islands of the sea and the whole earth were moved by God to make contribution to the host. The sadness was for those who remained behind, the joy for those who departed."

(63; original 357): This is upon the testimony of Albert of Aachen and Guibert. See Röhricht, Erster Kreuzzug, 240 sq., and references there given.

(64; original 358): Mannheimer, Die Judenverfolgungen in Speier, Worms und Mainz im Jahre 1096, während des ersten Kreuzzuges, Darmstadt, 1877. Hagenmeyer, p. 139, clears Peter of Amiens of the shameful glory of initiating this racial massacre, and properly claims it for count Emich and his mob. See also Röhricht, Gesch. d. ersten Kreuzzuges, 41-46.

(65; original 359): Albert of Aachen, II. 18.

(66; original 360): Gibbon calls him "a respectable prelate alike qualified for this world and the next."

(67; original 361): Bouillon, not to be confounded with Boulogne-sur-mer, on the English Channel, is a town in Belgian Luxemburg, and was formerly the capital of the lordship of Bouillon, which Godfrey mortgaged to the bishop of Liège in 1095. It has belonged to Belgium since 1831.

(68; original 362): Gibbon: "In the accomplished character of Tancred we discover all the virtues of a perfect knight, the true spirit of chivalry, which inspired the generous sentiments and social offices of man far better than the base philosophy, or the baser religion, of the time."

(69; original 363): Fulcher, I. 13, Rec., III. 336.

(70; original 364): Raymund of Agiles says Alexius treated the crusading army in such wise that so "long as ever he lives, the people will curse him and call him a traitor."

(71; original 365): The contemporary authorities represent the reprimand as given to Carpentarius. As Hagenmeyer suggests, Peter was included and Carpentarius’name alone mentioned because he was of royal blood.

(72; original 366): Among those who helped to dig for the weapon was Raymund of Agiles. Its authenticity was a matter of dispute, Adhemar being one of those who doubted. Barthelemy went through the ordeal of fire to prove the truth of his statements, but died in consequence of the injuries he suffered.

(73; original 367): According to Robert the Monk, IV., Rec., III. 824, a heavenly sign was granted on the eve of the final attack, a flame burning in the western sky, ignis de coelo veniens ab occidente. One of the interesting remains of the crusadal period are two letters written by Stephen, count of Chartres, to his wife Adele, the one before Nicaea and the other during the siege of Antioch. They are given in Hagenmeyer, Epistulae, pp. 138, 149.

(74; original 368): The figures are differently given. See Sybel, 412, and Röhricht, Gesch. des ersten Kreuzzuges, 183. William of Tyre gives the number as twenty-one thousand, and the army defending Jerusalem as forty thousand.

(75; original 369): Raymund of Agiles reports that the Crusaders forgot the exhortation of Peter Barthelemy to make the last part of the journey barefoot. "They remembered their weariness no more, and hastening their steps reached the walls amidst tears and praises."

(76; original 370): On this occasion Peter the Hermit and Arnulf, afterwards archbishop of Jerusalem, made addresses on the Mount of Olives to restore unity among the crusading leaders, especially Tancred and Raymund. Albert of Aachen, VI. 8, Rec., IV. 471, says, ad populos sermones ... plurimam discordiam quae inter Peregrinos de diversis causis excreverat exstinxerunt. Tancred had stirred up much jealousy by raising his banner over Bethlehem. Hagenmeyer, p. 259, accepts Albert’s account as genuine against Sybel.

(77; original 371): Miles splendidus et refulgens.

(78; original 372): Guibert, VII. 7, Rec., IV. 226; Robert the Monk, VII., Rec., III. 867.

(79; original 373): So Raymund of Agiles, an eyewitness, usque ad genua et usque ad frenos equorum, XX., Rec, III. 300. This he calls "the righteous judgment of God."

(80; original 374): So the Gesta: tales occisiones de paganorum gente nullus unquam audivit nec vidit ... nemo scit numerum eorum nisi solus deus. The slain are variously estimated from forty thousand to one hundred thousand. Guibert, Gesta, VII. 7, Rec., IV. 227, further says that in the temple area there was such a sea of blood, sanguinis unda, as almost to submerge the pedestrian.

(81; original 375): IX., Rec., III. 869. Robert gives an awful picture of the streets filled with dismembered bodies and running with gore.

(82; original 376): William of Tyre is the earliest witness to this scene. Leaving out embellishments, it does not seem to be at all unnatural. Hagenmeyer, pp. 265-269, calls it the "sheer invention of William’s fancy."

(83; original 377): Sybel, Gesch. des ersten Kreuzzugs, p. ii.

(84; original 378): Hagenmeyer, Peter der Eremite, p. 102, says, Dem Papste allein ist der Ruhm zu erhalten den ihm der Einsiedler von Amiens bis auf unsere Tage zur grösseren Hälfte streitig gemacht hat. Also Sybel, p. 243.

(85; original 379): Report of the Am. Hist. Association, 1900, p. 504 sq. See also the very emphatic statements of G. L.. Burr in art. The year 1000 and the Antecedents of the Crusades in the "Am. Hist. Rev.," April, 1901, pp. 429-439, and Trans. and Reprints of the Univ. of Pa., 1894, pp. 19 sqq.

(86; original 380): The Speech of Urban II. etc., in "Am. Hist. Rev.," 1906, p. 232.

(87; original 381): He says he reports what he heard, ex auditu et relatione.

(88; original 382): Nach einer solchen Katastrophe war ofenbar auch bei diesen alles Ansehen für ihn dabei, Hagenmeyer, p. 204.

(89; original 383): Ut stellae quoque juxta Apocalypsim de coelo cadere viderentur, Petrus ille, etc.

(90; original 384): Ekkehard XIII., Rec., V. 21, says that Peter’s cohorts became the object of derision to the Turks as soon as they reached Asia Minor, cohortes ...paganis fuerant jam ludibrio factae.

(91; original 385): Hagenmeyer, pp. 220 sqq., 243, suggests that at the time of William’s writing such things were no longer told.

(92; original 386): The official title of the kings was rex Latinorum in Hierusalem. In rejecting the crown, says William of Tyre, "Godfrey did so as a believing prince. He was the best of kings, the light and mirror of all others,"lumen et speculum, IX. 9, Rec., I. 377. The clergy had dreamed of the complete subjection of the civil government of Jerusalem to the spiritual government under the patriarch. The first patriarch not only secured for his jurisdiction one-fourth of Jerusalem and Jaffa, but the promise from Godfrey of the whole of both cities, provided Godfrey was successful in taking Cairo or some other large hostile city, or should die without male heirs. See Röhricht, Gesch. des ersten Kreuzzuges, p. 218.

(93; original 387): See Dagobert’s appeal in Hagenmeyer, Epistulae, 176 sq., 412 sqq. He speaks of "Jerusalem as the most excellent of all places for sanctity," and says that "for this reason it was oppressed by the pagans and infidels." Fulcher, writing of the year 1100, declares that there were only three hundred knights and as many footmen left for the defence of Jerusalem, Jaffa, and Ramleh. See quotation in Hagenmeyer, 415.

(94; original 388): Hic jacet inclitus dux Godefridus de Bouillon qui totam sitam terram acquisivit cultui christiano, cujus anima regnet cum Christo.

(95; original 389): According to Raymund of Agiles, Arnulf was a man of loose life and his amours subjects of camp songs.

(96; original 390): From the fall of Acre, 1291 to 1848, the patriarchs, with two exceptions, lived in Rome. In 1848 Valerga, appointed patriarch by Pius IX., took up his residence in Jerusalem.

(97; original 391): Wilken devotes a long treatment to the subject, I. pp. 307-424.

(98; original 392): Fulani, "anybodies." The designation fulan ibn fulan, "so and so, the son of so and so," is a most opprobrious mode of address among the Arabs.

(99; original 393): The following mode of reducing a tribe of robbers is characteristic. The robbers took refuge in a cave. Baldwin resorted to smoking them out. Two emerged; Baldwin spoke kindly to them, dressed one up and sent him back with fair promises, while he put the other to death. Ten others emerged. One was sent back and the other nine put to death. The same method was employed till two hundred and thirty had been induced to come forth and were put to death. The fires were then started again till all came forth and met the same fate.

(100; original 394): From this point William of Tyre writes as an eye-witness, XVI. sqq.

(101; original 395): According to the letter of Terricius, Master of the Temple, two hundred and ninety Templars perished, and the Saracens covered the whole land from Tyre to Gaza like swarms of ants. Richard of Hoveden, an. 1187, says the Templars fought like lions.

(102; original 396): Saladin offered a glass of water to Guy. When Guy handed It to Reginald, Saladin exclaimed, "I did not order that. You gave it," and at once despatched Reginald by his own hand, or through a servant. Reginald had plundered a caravan in which Saladin’s sister was travelling. Lane-Poole, Saladin, p. 215.

(103; original 397): The bezant was worth three dollars.

(104; original 398): See Otto of Freising, VII. 30.

(105; original 399): Gottlob, Kreuzablass, 106 sqq. Eugenius quoted Urban II’s decree of indulgence at Clermont.

(106; original 400): De consideratione, II. 1, Reinkens’translation, pp. 31-37. In this chapter of his famous tract, Bernard explains and justifies his course in the Crusade.

(107; original 401): Odo, I. 1, caeperunt undique conclamando cruces expetere ... coactus est vestes suas in cruces scindere et seminare.

(108; original 402): As a proof of Konrad’s strength, William of Tyre, XVII. 4, relates that at the siege of Damascus he hewed a man clad in armor through head, neck, and shoulder to the armpit with one stroke of his blade.

(109; original 403): Bk. XVI. 20. William suggests that Manuel’s jealousy was aroused because Konrad asserted the title, king of the Romans. Diehl, Essays on the Crusades, p. 107, doubts the statement that Manuel’s guides intentionally misled and betrayed the Germans. He, however, acknowledges that Greek inhabitants of Asia Minor "fleeced or starved the Latins."

(110; original 404): William of Tyre, XVII., gives a list of the distinguished personages present, Bishop Otto of Freising, the emperor’s brother, being among them.

(111; original 405): De consideratione, II. 1.

(112; original 406): The story of Richard’s seizing a lion and tearing out its throbbing heart was a subject of English romance in the fourteenth century and probably of French romance in the thirteenth century.

(113; original 407): It required at least fifteen days for a ship to go from Acre to Marseilles, and about the same time for news to reach Rome from Jerusalem. The indulgences offered to Crusaders by Alexander III., on the news of Saladin’s conquests in Egypt and his defeat of the Christians at Banias, 1181, are quoted by Gottlob, 119 sq. Alexander appealed to the examples of Urban II, and Eugenius III.

(114; original 408): He sold the archbishopric of York for 3,000 pounds. Henry is reported to have left 900,000 pounds in gold and silver. Rog. of Wendover, an. 1180.

(115; original 409): Richard of Devizes, X.

(116; original 410): Giraldus Cambrensis accompanied the archbishop and gathered the materials for his itinerary on the way.

(117; original 411): Frederick announced his expedition in a letter to Saladin, in which he enumerated the tribes that were to take part in it, from the "tall Bavarian" to the sailors of Venice and Pisa. See Itin. reg. Ricardi de Hoveden, etc.

(118; original 412): Ranke, VIII. 246 sqq., spicily speculates upon the possible consequences of Isaac’s dethronement, and, as a German, regrets that Frederick did not take the prize, Es war ein Moment das nicht so leicht wieder kommen konnte.

(119; original 413): Another account by one who accompanied the expedition was that in his impatience to proceed, Barbarossa strove to swim the river and was drowned. Ranke, VIII. 249, regards the view taken in the text as the better one.

(120; original 414): Itinerary, III. 16.

(121; original 415): Richard’s fleet, when he sailed from Messina, consisted of one hundred and fifty large ships and fifty-three galleys.

(122; original 416): The Itinerary, III. 2, says Richard’s arrival was welcomed with transports of joy, shoutings, and blowing of trumpets. He was taken ashore as if the desired of all nations had come, and the night was made so bright with wax torches and flaming lights "that it seemed to be usurped by the brightness of the day, and the Turks thought the whole valley was on fire." Richard of Devizes, LXIII., says, "The besiegers received Richard with as much joy as if it had been Christ who had come again."

(123; original 417): The Itinerary, I., 66, says Baldwin was made sick unto death when he saw "the army altogether dissolute and given up to drinking, women, and dice."

(124; original 418): The loss before Acre was very heavy. The Itinerary gives a list of 6 archbishops, 12 bishops, 40 counts, and 500 knights who lost their lives. IV. 6. De Hoveden also gives a formidable list, in which are included the names of the dukes of Swabia, Flanders, and Burgundy, the archbishops of Besançon, Arles, Montreal, etc. Baldwin died Nov. 19, 1190. The Itinerary compares the siege of Acre to the siege of Troy, and says. (I. 32) "it would certainly obtain eternal fame as a city for which the whole world contended."

(125; original 419): The Itinerary and other documents make frequent reference to its deadly use. Among the machines used on both sides were the petrariae, which hurled stones, and mangonels used for hurling stones and other missiles. Itinerary, III. 7, etc. One of the grappling machines was called a "cat." The battering ram was also used, and the sow, a covering under which the assailants made their approach to the walls. King Richard was an expert in the use of the arbalest, or cross-bow.

(126; original 420): The price of a loaf of bread rose from a penny to 40 shillings, and a horseload of corn was sold for 60 marks. De Hoveden, etc. Horse flesh was greedily eaten, even to the intestines, which were sold for 10 sols. Even grass was sought after to appease hunger. A vivid description of the pitiful sufferings from famine is given in the Itinerary, I. 67-83.

(127; original 421): Itinerary, I. 44.

(128; original 422): This pretext is upon the sole authority of de Hoveden, an. 1191. He says, however, that Saladin did not execute the Christian captives until Richard had declined to withdraw his threat and to give more time for the payment of the ransom money and the delivery of the true cross. Archer, Hist. of the Crusades, p. 331, thinks that Baba-ed-din’s account implies Saladin’s massacre; but Lane-Poole, Life of Saladin, p. 307, is of the contrary opinion. The Itinerary, IV. 4, states that Richard’s followers, leapt forward to fulfil his commands, thankful to the divine grace for the permission to take such vengeance for the Christians whom the captives had slain with bolts and arrows."It has nothing to say of a massacre by Saladin. Lane-Poole, carried away by admiration for Saladin, takes occasion at this point to say that " in the struggle of the Crusades the virtues of civilization, magnanimity, toleration, real chivalry, and gentle culture were an on the side of the Saracens."The duke of Burgundy was party to the massacre of the Turkish captives.

(129; original 423): Itinerary, VI. 23. Here is a description of one of Richard’s frequent frays as given in the Itinerary, VI. 4: "Richard was conspicuous above all the rest by his royal bearing. He was mounted on a tall charger and charged the enemy singly. His ashen lance was shivered by his repeated blows; but instantly drawing his sword, he pressed upon the fugitive Turks and mowed them down, sweeping away the hindmost and subduing the foremost. Thus he thundered on, cutting and hewing. No kind of armor could resist his blows, for the edge of his sword cut open the heads from the top to the teeth. Thus waving his sword to and fro, he scared away the routed Turks as a wolf when he pursues the flying sheep."

(130; original 424): De Joinville, Life of St. Louis , an. 1253, says no doubt with the truth that Richard would have taken Jerusalem but for the envy and treachery of the Duke of Burgundy. He repeats the saying of Richard, which is almost too good not to be true. When an officer said, "Sire, come here and I will show you Jerusalem," the king throwing down his arms and looking up to heaven exclaimed, "I pray thee, O Lord God, that I may never look on the Holy City until I can deliver it from thy enemies." The Itinerary has nothing to say on the subject. Richard of Devizes, XC., states that Hubert, bishop of Salisbury, after his pilgrimage to Jerusalem, urged the king to go in as a pilgrim, but that "the worthy indignation of his noble mind would not consent to receive from the courtesy of the Gentiles what he could not obtain by the gift of God."

(131; original 425): Baha-ed-din, as quoted by Lane-Poole, p. 354. De Hoveden speaks of fruits, the Itinerary of horses. Later story ascribes to Saladin a yearly grant of one thousand bezants of gold to the Knights of St. John at Acre. In order to test the charity of the knights, the sultan had gone to the hospital in disguise and found the reports of their merciful treatment well founded. Of this and of the story of his knighthood at the hands of Humphrey of Toron, and vouched for by the contemporary Itinerary of King Richard, the Arab authorities know nothing. See Lane-Poole,Life of Saladin, 387 sqq.

(132; original 426): A western legend given by Vincent de Beauvais relates that as Saladin was dying he called to him his standard-bearer and bade him carry through the streets of Damascus the banner of his death as he had carried the banner of his wars; namely, a rag attached to a lance, and cry out. "Lo, at his death, the king of the East can take nothing with him but this cloth only."

(133; original 427): TheItinerary gives a story of Saladin and the notorious miracle of the holy fire until recently shown in the church of the Holy Sepulchre. It may well be true. When Saladin, on one occasion, saw the holy flame descend and light a lamp, he ordered the lamp blown out to show it was a fraud. But it was immediately rekindled as if by a miracle. Extinguished a second and a third time, it was again and again rekindled. "Oh, what use is it to resist the invisible Power!" exclaims the author of the Itinerary, V. 16.

(134; original 428): Hurter regards the numbers handed down as greatly exaggerated.

(135; original 429): An epigram, dwelling upon the folly of the movement, ran:—

"Ad mare stultorum

Tendebat iter puerorum ."

"To the sea of the fools

Led the path of the children."

(136; original 430): Wilken for this assertion quotes theHistory of the Genoese Senate and People, by Peter Bizari, Antwerp, 1679. One of the families was the house of the Vivaldi.

(137; original 431): See Wilken, VI. 83.

(138; original 432): So Wilken, Sie ist durch die Zeugnisse glaubwürdiger Geschichtschreiber so fest begründet, dass ihre Wahrheit nicht bezweifelt werden kann, p. 72. Röhricht, Hist. Zeitschrift, XXXVI. 5, also insists upon the historical genuineness of the reports.

(139; original 433): See the ample description of Hurter, I. pp. 221-230, etc.

(140; original 434): Epp. of Innocent, I. 353, 354, etc., Migne, 214, 329 sqq.

(141; original 435): Ep. I. 353, Migne, 214, 325 sqq.

(142; original 436): Guntherus, Migne, 212, 225.

(143; original 437): A French translation of Innocent’s letter commissioning Fulke to preach the Crusade is given by Charasson, p. 99.

(144; original 438): Thibaut, then twenty-two, and Louis, then twenty-seven, were nephews of the king of France, Villehardouin, 3; Wailly’s ed., p. 5. Thibaut died before the Crusaders started from France.

(145; original 439): Villehardouin, who was one of the six members of the commission (Wailly’s ed., p. 11), says, "The Turks could be more easily destroyed there than in any other country." Egypt was often called by the Crusaders, "the land of Babylon."

(146; original 440): Wailly’s edition of Villehardouin, p. 452, makes the sum 4,420,000 francs. It reckons a mark as the equivalent of 52 francs. The Grand Council added fifty armed galleys "for the love of God," on condition that during the continuance of the alliance Venice should have one-half the spoils of conquest.

(147; original 441): Villehardouin describes him as a man de bien grand coeur. He died at ninety-seven, in 1205, and was buried in the Church of St. Sophia. In his reply to the deputation, the doge recognized the high birth of the Crusaders in the words, "we perceive that the lords are in the highest rank of those who do not wear a crown" (Villehardouin, 16; Wailly’s ed., 13).

(148; original 442): Villehardouin, 56 sqq.; Wailly’s ed., 33 sq.

(149; original 443): Villehardouin mentions only the proposition to go against Zara. Robert of Clary and other writers state that Dandolo made a previous proposition that the fleet should proceed to Mohammedan territory and that the first booty should be used to pay the Crusaders’debt. He then substituted the proposition to go against Zara, and the Crusaders were forced by their circumstances to accept. There is some ground for the charge that in May, 1202, Dandolo made a secret treaty with the sultan of Egypt. See Pears, 271 sqq.

(150; original 444): Villehardouin and Robert de Clary. Clary’s account is very vivacious and much the more detailed of the two.

(151; original 445): A deputation afterwards visited Innocent and secured his absolution, Villehardouin, 107; Wailly’s ed., 61. The news of the death of Fulke of Neuilly reached the Crusaders on the eve of their breaking away from Venice. Villehardouin, 73; Wailly’s ed., 43, calls him le bon, le saint homme.

(152; original 446): Villehardouin, 109. Pears, p. 268, speaks pathetically of the Crusaders as "about to commit the great crime of the Middle Ages, by the destruction of the citadel against which the hitherto irresistible wave of Moslem invasion had beaten and been broken." Not praiseworthy, it is true, was the motive of the Crusaders, yet there is no occasion for bemoaning the fate of Constantinople and the Greeks. The conquest of the Latins prolonged the successful resistance to the Turks.

(153; original 447): Arabs were allowed to live in the city and granted the privileges of their religious rites. Gibbon with characteristic irony says. "The Flemish pilgrims were scandalized by the aspect of a mosque or a synagogue in which one God was worshipped without a partner or a son."

(154; original 448): Villehardouin, 233, Wailly’s ed. p. 137, pronounces the capture of Constantinople one of the most difficult feats ever undertaken, une des plus redoutables choses à faire qui jamais fut. A city of such strong fortifications the Franks had not seen before.

(155; original 449): Hurter (I. p. 685), comparing the conquest of Constantinople with the capture of Jerusalem, exalts the piety of Godfrey and the first Crusaders over against the Venetians and their greed for booty. He forgot the awful massacre in Jerusalem.

(156; original 450): Reg., VIII. Ep., 133.

(157; original 451): Nicetas gives a list of these losses. See Gibbon, LX., and Hurter.

(158; original 452): Villehardouin, 191; Wailly’s ed., 111, says des reliques it n’en faut point parler, car en ce jour il y en avait autant dans la ville que dans le reste du monde. The account of Guntherus, Migne, 212, 253 sqq., is the most elaborate. His informant the Abbot Martin, was an insatiable relic hunter.

(159; original 453): See Riant; Hurter, I. 694-702; Pears, 365-370. A volume would scarce contain the history, real and legendary, of these objects of veneration.

(160; original 454): A curious account is given by Dalmatius of Sergy, of his discovery of the head of St. Clement in answer to prayer, and the deception he practised in making away with it. The relic went to Cluny and was greatly prized. See Hurter. The successful stealth of Abbot Martin is told at length by the German Guntherus, Migne, 212, 251 sq.

(161; original 455): Matthew Paris, in his account, says, "It was precious beyond gold or topaz, and to the credit of the French kingdom, and indeed, of all the Latins, it was solemnly and devoutly received in grand procession amidst the ringing of bells and the devout prayers of the faithful followers of Christ, and was placed in the king’s chapel in Paris." Luard’s ed., IV. 75; Giles’s trans., I. 311.

(162; original 456): The mode of election was fixed before the capture of the city, Villehardouin, 234, 256-261; Wailly’s ed., 137,152 sqq. The election took place in a chamber of the palace. The leader of the French forces, Boniface of Montferrat, married the widow of the emperor Isaac and was made king of Salonica. Innocent III. (VIII. 134, Migne, 215, 714) congratulated Isaac’s widow upon her conversion to the Latin Church.

(163; original 457): He wrote to Baldwin that, while it was desirable the Eastern Church should be subdued, he was more concerned that the Holy Land should be rescued. He urged him and the Venetians to eat the bread of repentance that they might fight the battle of the Lord with a pure heart.

(164; original 458): The Greek patriarch had left the city reduced to a state of apostolic poverty, of which Gibbon, LXI, says that "had it been voluntary it might perhaps have been meritorious."

(165; original 459): Pears concludes his work, The Fall of Constantinople, by the false judgment that the effects of the Fourth Crusade were altogether disastrous for civilization. He surmises that, but for it, the city would never have fallen into the hands of the Turks, and the Sea of Marmora and the Black Sea would now be surrounded by "prosperous and civilized nations," pp. 412 sqq. There was no movement of progress in the Byzantine empire for the Crusaders to check.

(166; original 460): Plenam suorum peccaminum veniam indulgemus. See Mansi, XXII. 1067; Mirbt, Quellen, 126, Gottlob, 137 sq.

(167; original 461): For the text of Frederick’s summons to his crusade of 1221, see Mathews, Select Med. Documents, 120 sq.

(168; original 462): Funk, in Wetzer-Welte, VII. 1166, says that in view of contemporary testimony, Frederick’s sickness cannot be doubted. Roger Wendover, an. 1227, however, doubted it. Funk is wrong in saying that it was not till 1239 that Gregory, aggravated by the emperor’s conduct, impeached Frederick’s plea of sickness. In his sentence of excommunication of 1228, Gregory asserted that Frederick II "was enticed away to the usual pleasures of his kingdom and made a frivolous pretext of bodily infirmity." In 1235, at a time when emperor and pope were reconciled, Gregory spoke of Jerusalem, "as being restored to our well-beloved son in Christ, Frederick."

(169; original 463): See Röhricht, Regesta regni Hier., 262, and Bréholles, III. 86-90.

(170; original 464): Geroldus was patriarch of Jerusalem and notified Gregory IX. of Frederick’s "fraudulent pact with the Egyptian sultan." Röhricht, 263.

(171; original 465): In 1240 a petition signed by German bishops and princes and addressed to Gregory urged him to cease from strife with Frederick as it interfered with a crusade. Bréholles, V. 985.

(172; original 466): Hist. Essays, I. 283-313.

(173; original 467): Bréholles, V. 327-340.

(174; original 468): "Piety was his ruling passion." Guizot, p. 117. De Joinville frequently calls him "the good king" and Matthew Paris "that most Christian king."

(175; original 469): See the account in a letter from the prelates of the Holy Land in Matthew Paris, an. 1244. The invaders were called Tartars by Robert, patriarch of Jerusalem, in his letter to Innocent IV. Röhriclit, Reg. regni Hier., p. 299.

(176; original 470): Joinville, accompanied by twenty knights, joined the king at Cyprus. He was a man of religious fervor, made pilgrimages to all the shrines in the vicinity of his castle before his departure, and never failed in his long absence to confine himself to bread and water on Fridays (History, an. 1250). One of his paragraphs gives a graphic insight into the grief which must have been felt by thousands of Crusaders as they left their homes for the long and uncertain journey to the East. It runs: "In passing near the castle of Joinville, I dared never turn my eyes that way for fear of feeling too great regret and lest my courage should fail on leaving my children and my fair castle of Joinville, which I loved in my heart."

(177; original 471): Joinville speaks of Louis having "as much trouble in keeping his own people together in time of peace as in the time of his ill fortunes."an. 1249.

(178; original 472): Within a stone’s throw of the king’s tent were several brothels. A curious punishment was prescribed by the king for a knight caught with a harlot at Acre. Joinville, pt. II. an. 1250, Bohn’s trans. 484.

(179; original 473): See the appalling description of Joinville, an. 1249.

(180; original 474): Joinville, an. 1250.

(181; original 475): Joinville, an. 1253.

(182; original 476): Joinville declined the king’s appeal to accompany him, and advised against the expedition on the ground of the peaceable state of France with the king at home, and of the king’s physical weakness which prevented him from wearing armor or sitting on horseback long at a time.

(183; original 477): Since 1881 a dependency of France.

(184; original 478): The sultan had agreed to pay yearly tribute to Roger II. In the treaty made at the close of the expedition, he agreed to make up the arrearages of tribute to Charles.

(185; original 479): M. Paris, an. 1271

(186; original 480): The question whether the king’s heart was deposited in the Sainte Chapelle at Paris or not, led to a spirited discussion in 1843. See Letronne, Examen critique de la découverte du pretendu coeur de St. Louis faite a la Sainte Chapelle le 15 Mai 1843, Paris, 1844; Lenormant, Preuves de la découverte du coeur de St. Louis, Paris, 1846.

(187; original 481): For a contemporary description of Acre, see Itin. regis Ricardi, I. 32.

(188; original 482): Com. in Jerem., see Neander, Ch. Hist., IV. 189 sqq., Engl. trans.

(189; original 483): Mansi, XXIV. 111-120.

(190; original 484): Contemplations of God. See Zwemer, Life of Raymund Lull, 52, 149.

(191; original 485): Enchiridion militis christiani, Methuen’s ed. 1905, p. 8 sq.

(192; original 486): No appellation was too degrading to give to the enemies of the cross. The most common one was dogs. The biographers of Richard I. have no compunction in relating in one line gifts made by Saracens and in the next calling them dogs. See Itin. Ricardi, etc. So Walter Map says sepulchrum et crux Domini praeda sunt canum quorum fames in tantum lassata fuit et sanguine martyrorum, etc., Wright’s ed., I. 15, p. 229.

(193; original 487): So Humbert de Romanis, 1274; Mansi, XXIV. 116. A sixth objection against the Crusades as stated and answered by him ran as follows: quod ex ista pugna non sequitur fructus spiritualis quia Saraceni magis convertuntur ad blasphemiam quam ad fidem; occisi autem ad infernum mittuntur, etc.

(194; original 488): II. 338, etc.

(195; original 489): Archer, p. 447, well says: "They raised mankind above the ignoble sphere of petty ambitions to seek after an ideal that was neither sordid nor selfish. They called forth all that was heroic in human nature, and filled the world with the inspiration of noble thoughts and deeds."

(196; original 490): Decline and Fall, LVIII.

(197; original 491): This is clearly apparent from the English and other mediaeval chronicles, such as the Chronicles of M. Paris, Hoveden, etc.

(198; original 492): The ships of the two great Military Orders alone carried great numbers of pilgrims. In 1182 one of their ships was wrecked on the Egyptian coast with 1500 pilgrims. In 1180 several vessels met the same fate, 2500 pilgrims were drowned and 1500 sold into slavery. In 1246 their ships carried from the port of Marseilles alone 6000 pilgrims. See Prutz in Essays, p. 54. This author, in laying weight upon the economic influences of the Crusades, says properly, that they "had only in part to do with religion, and particularly with the church," p. 77. Arabic words, such as damask, tarif, and bazar, were introduced into the vocabularies of European nations, and products, such as saffron, maize, melons, and little onions, were carried back by the Crusaders. The transfer of money made necessary the development of the system of letters of credit.

(199; original 493): The Crusades, said the eloquent Dr. Richard S. Storrs, Bernard of Clairvaux, p. 558, furnished "as truly an ideal enthusiasm as that of any one who has sought to perform his missionary work in distant lands or has wrought into permanent laws and Institutions the principles of equity and the temper of love. And they must forever remain an example resplendent and shining of what an enthusiasm that is careless of obstacles and fearless of danger can accomplish."

(200; original 494): At the battle of Gaza with the Chorasmians, 1244, of two hundred and sixteen Knights of St. John who entered the battle, two hundred remained dead on the field.

(201; original 495): After the battle of Tiberias, the Knights of St. John, for a few years, made their strong fortress, Margat, the base of their operations.

(202; original 496): See M. Paris, an. 1259. The famous antithesis of Gibbon (chap. LVIII.) pleases the ear and contains some truth, but makes a wrong impression. "The Knights of the Temple and St. John neglected to live, but they prepared to die in the service of Christ."

(203; original 497): The synod of Salzburg, 1292, decided in favor of the union.

(204; original 498): Fratres hospitalis S. Johannis, Hospitalarii, Johannitae, milites hospitalis S. Johannis. From the fourteenth century they were also known as the Knights of Rhodes and from the sixteenth as the Knights of Malta. For a list of the houses of the female members of this order, Le Roulx, Les Hospitaliers, 300 sq.

(205; original 499): The bull of Pascal, II. 1113, speaks of the hospital in Jerusalem adjoining the church of the Baptist, xenodochium ... juxta Beati Johannis Baptistae ecclesiam.

(206; original 500): William of Tyre, XVIII. 5; de Vitry, Hist. Jerus., 64. The Mary, whose name the convent bore, was Mary Magdalene.

(207; original 501): Le Roulx, Les Hospitaliers, 33, connects the order with the hospital founded by Maurus,nous croyons pouvoir persister à penser que les Amalfitans furent les précurseurs des Hospitaliers

(208; original 502): William of Tyre, VII. 23, states that he was held in chains during the siege of Jerusalem.

(209; original 503): See Le Roulx, pp. 44 sqq. Gerard is called in an old chronicle "Guardian of the hospital of the poor in Jerusalem," guardianus hospitalis pauperum, etc., Hurter, IV. 315, note

(210; original 504): Woodhouse, p. 20, gives a list of no less than fifty-four houses belonging to the Hospital in England.

(211; original 505): The bull in Mansi, XXI. 780.

(212; original 506): They were monks. The order had no priests until the time of Alexander III., who gave it the right to receive priests and clerics. Priests became necessary in order that the new custom might be followed which gave to priests alone the right of absolution. During the first century of their existence, the members of military orders made confession of their sins in the open chapters and were punished at the order of the Master by public scourging or otherwise. The strict church law of confession and of absolution by the priest was not defined till later by the Fourth Lateran Council, and Thomas Aquinas. See Lea, The Absolution Formula of the Templars.

(213; original 507): Le Roulx, 290 sq.

(214; original 508): For the formula of admission, see Le Roulx, 288 sq.

(215; original 509): See Uhlhorn for the amount of linen and other goods expected from the various houses in Europe. There was a female branch of the order of which, however, very little is known. In 1188 Sancha, queen of Aragon, founded a rich convent for it at Sixena near Saragossa.

(216; original 510): On October 31, 1898, the emperor William II. of Germany, while on a visit to Jerusalem, dedicated the Protestant church of the Redeemer, built on the ancient site of the hospital of the Knights of St. John, opposite the church of the Holy Sepulchre.

(217; original 511): Templarii, fratres militiae templi, equites templarii, pauperes commilitiones Christi templique Salamonis, are some of the titles by which they were known. There was not nearly as much resemblance between the Hospitallers and Templars as between the Templars and Teutonic knights. Curzon, p. xi.

(218; original 512): William of Tyre. See Hefele, V. 401 sq.

(219; original 513): De laude novae militiae.

(220; original 514): On St. Bernard’s services to the order, see the biographies by Morison, 141 sqq., and Storrs, 567-574.

(221; original 515): In England they settled at the old Temple outside of Holborn, whence they removed to the new Temple on the Thames, 1185. The Temple church was completed in 1240. M. Paris gives an account of the dedication and the banquet which was provided by the Hospitallers. Stephen and his queen gave the Templars several places about 1150. Woodhouse, p. 260, gives a list of twenty-seven English houses.

(222; original 516): An. 1244.

(223; original 517): At the end of the thirteenth century. This is the estimate of de Chambure. Schottmüller estimates them at 40,000,000 francs. William of Tyre, XII. 7, speaks of their possessions as "immense." Their wealth and greed were proverbial.

(224; original 518): Funk calls Alexander’s bull the Magna Charta of the order. Wetzer-Welte, XI. 1315.

(225; original 519): With reference to 1 Pet. 5:8, Curzon, 58.

(226; original 520): Non nobis, Domine, non nobis sed tuo nomini da gloriam.

(227; original 521): Curzon, XXVII.

(228; original 522): Fugiat feminae oscula Christi militia, Mansi, XXI. 72; also Schnürer, 153.

(229; original 523): Schnürer, Rule XI. p. 138.

(230; original 524): M. Paris, Luard’s ed., IV. 337 sqq., gives the letters from the patriarch of Jerusalem and the vice-master of the Temple, 1244. This chronicler is very severe upon the Templars for their arrogant pride and their jealous rivalry of the Hospitallers. An example of this jealousy was their refusal to accompany King Amalric to Egypt because to the Hospitallers had been assigned first place.

(231; original 525): Among their fortresses was the castle Pilgrim near Acre, built 1218, whose great size and splendor are described by James de Vitry.

(232; original 526): The houses of the order became important money centres in France and England in the thirteenth century, and furnished to kings, bishops, and nobles a safety-deposit for funds and treasures of plate, jewels, and important records. Henry III. and other English kingss borrowed from them, as did also French kings. The Templars also acted as disbursers for monies loaned by Italian bankers or as trustees for other monies, as, for example, the annual grant of one thousand marks promised by John to his sister-in-law, Berengaria. John frequently stopped at the house of the Templars in London. See Cunningham, Growth of English Industries and Commerce, 3d ed. Leopold Delisle, Les operationsfinancières des Templiers, Paris, 1889. Eleanor Ferris, Financial Relations of the Knights Templars to the English Crown, in "Am. Hist. Rev.," October, 1902.

(233; original 527): Charasson, quoting Richard de Hoveden, Vie de Foulques de Neuilly, 89 sq.

(234; original 528): Deutscher Orden, Ordo S. Mariae Theutonicorum.

(235; original 529): Under the name domus hospitalis S. Mariae Theutonicorum in Jerusalem. A German hospital was dedicated in Jerusalem to St. Mary, 1128.

(236; original 530): At the council of Constance, 1416, the king of Poland protested against their right to convert by the sword.

(237; original 531): In the conflict of Lewis the Bavarian with the papacy, the Teutonic order espoused the emperor’s cause and received from him important gifts and privileges.

(238; original 532): Fratres militiae Christi, gladiferi, a military order founded in 1202.

(239; original 533): Kleider aus, Kleider an, Essen, Trinken, Schlafengehen, ist die Arbeit so die Deutsche Herren han.

(240; original 534): Luther in 1523 wrote a tract calling upon the Teutonic knights to abandon their false rule of celibacy and to practise the true chastity of marriage. Ermahnung an die Herren Deutschen Ordens falsche Keuschheit zu meiden und zur rechten ehelichen Keuschheit zu greifen. Albrecht introduced the Lutheran reformation into Brandenburg. He married the Danish princess Dorothea.

(241; original 535): Several orders combining military and religious vows existed in Spain and Portugal and did service against the Moors. The order of Iago of Campostella received the papal sanction in 1175 and protected pilgrims to the shrine of Campostella. The order of Calatrava received papal approval 1164, and took an active part in the struggle against the Moors. The order of Alcantara was recognized by Lucius III., 1183. The headship of the last two bodies was transferred to the crown under Ferdinand the Catholic.

  1. 吉本 (Gibbon) 鄙视十字军东征，视其为宗教狂热的一场无益表演，却又称之为“世界的大争辩”，第LIX章。↩︎

  2. 约翰·菲斯克 (John Fiske)：《美洲的发现》(Discovery of America)，I. 318、419、505。↩︎

  3. 记述第三次十字军东征的《理查一世行程记》(The Itinerary of Richard I.)，着重叙述高级教士和神职人员善战的素质。书中提到一位司祭，他不断奋力抗敌，不知疲倦地用投石器投射矢石，I. 42。贝桑松 (Besançon) 大主教督造了一座用来撞毁阿卡城墙的大型器械，并承担其费用，I. 60。坎特伯雷大主教鲍德温 (Baldwin of Canterbury) 虽已年老，麾下仍有二百名骑士和三百名随从；“修院院长和主教们各率自己的部队，为信仰奋勇作战”，I. 62。↩︎

  4. 《论圣殿骑士》(De militibus templi)，V.；米涅 (Migne)，182，928。↩︎

  5. 温多弗的罗杰 (Roger of Wendover)，卢阿德 (Luard) 编本，M. Paris，III: 35。↩︎

  6. Milites Christi（基督的战士），修士罗伯特 (Robert the Monk)，VII.，Rec.，III. 867；Christi Militia（基督的军队），吉贝尔 (Guibert)，VII.，II.，Rec.，IV. 229。这支军队也称为 crucifer exercitus（负十字架的军队），埃克哈德 (Ekkehard)，Rec. V. 16。↩︎

  7. 法语用语是 se croiser, prendre la croix, prendre le signe de la croix（佩上十字、领受十字架、领受十字标记）。例如，见维尔阿杜安 (Villehardouin)，2、8、18，瓦伊 (Wailly) 编本，第3、7、13页。这位第四次十字军东征的史家也称十字军为 les croisés（佩十字者），38，瓦伊编本，第24页。↩︎

  8. Quoniam illi, qui cum pietate catholicae religionis in belli certamine cadunt, requies eos aeternae vitae suscipiet contra paganos atque infideles strenue dimicantes，等等（因为那些怀着对大公信仰的虔诚，在战斗中奋勇抵抗异教徒和不信者而阵亡的人，必蒙接纳，得享永生的安息）。戈特洛布 (Gottlob)：《十字军赎罪券》(Kreuzablass)，25。↩︎

  9. Quicumque pro sola devotione ...ad liberandam ecclesiam Dei Jerusalem profectus fuerit, iter illud pro omni paenitentia reputetur（凡纯出于虔诚……前往耶路撒冷解救上帝教会的人，此次旅程应抵作其全部补赎）。戈特洛布 (Gottlob)，72及后；米尔布特 (Mirbt)：《史料》(Quellen)，114。↩︎

  10. 《事迹》(Gesta)，I. 1；Rec.，IV. 124。↩︎

  11. 利 (Lea) 在《宗教裁判所史》(Hist. of Inquis.)，I. 44中说：“十字军被列入教士之列，只受教会司法的管辖，因此既免于属世的审判，也免于属天的审判。”↩︎

  12. 见伊迪丝·C. 布兰博尔 (Edith C. Bramball)：《十字军世俗特权的起源》(Origin of the Temporal Privileges of Crusaders)，载《美国神学杂志》(Am Jour. of Theol.)，1901年，第279—292页；以及戈特洛布 (Gottlob)：《十字军赎罪券》(Kreuzablass)，第140页及后。↩︎

  13. 《论圣殿骑士》(De militibus templi)，II.、III.；米涅 (Migne)，182，923及下页。↩︎

  14. 富尔彻 (Fulcher) 将 nunc jure contra barbaros pugnent qui olim fratres dimicabant（从前与弟兄交战的人，如今可以正当地与蛮族作战）这句话放在乌尔班 (Urban) 口中时，表达的正是这个意思，Rec.，III. 323。二百年后，阿尔瓦鲁斯·佩拉吉乌斯 (Alvarus Pelagius) 提出了同样的论点：quamvis Saraceni Palestinam possident, juste tamen exinde depelluntur（撒拉森人虽占有巴勒斯坦，将他们逐出那里仍是正当的），等等。见施瓦布 (Schwab)：《约翰·格尔森》(Joh. Gerson)，26。↩︎

  15. 《神学大全》(Summa)，II. (2)，188，3；米涅 (Migne)，III.，1366及下页：militare propter aliquid mundanum est omni religioni contrarium, non autem militare propter obsequium Dei（为某种世俗目的作战，有违一切修道生活；但为事奉上帝而作战则不然），等等。他补充说，神职人员参战，必须服从君主或教会的命令，不可自行其是。↩︎

  16. 卢阿德 (Luard) 编本，V. 196。↩︎

  17. 多尔的鲍德里克 (Baldric of Dol)：《耶路撒冷史》(Hist. Jerus.)，I. 8；Rec.，IV. 17：gaudebant uxores abeuntibus maritis dilectissimis（妻子们因至爱的丈夫出征而欢喜），等等。↩︎

  18. 海斯特巴赫的凯撒 (Caesar of Heisterbach) 在《对话录》(Dial.)，X. 22中提到一位妇女，分娩时痛苦不堪，但她一同意丈夫参加十字军东征，便顺利生产了。↩︎

  19. “法兰克人” (Franks) 成为东方对欧洲人的通称，至今仍然如此。十字军的热忱直到十二世纪才充分感染德国。豪克 (Hauck)：《德国教会史》(Kirchengesch. Deutschlands)，IV. 80。↩︎

  20. 这个说法译自拉丁文 ultra mare（海外），用来指东方；据我所知，最早使用它的是格列高利七世 (Gregory VII.)，见《书信登记册》(Reg.)，II. 37；米涅 (Migne)，148，390。↩︎

  21. 格雷戈罗维乌斯 (Gregorovius)，IV. 288，说在罗马找不到热忱的痕迹。“倘若乌尔班号召元老院与人民在宗教热忱中奋起，离开罗马的废墟，前去解救耶路撒冷，他们大概只会报以讥笑。”十字军东征使朝圣者的行程由使徒的坟墓转向救主的坟墓，因而给罗马造成了经济损失。↩︎

  22. 这里有这样一个神迹。1177年拉姆拉 (Ramleh) 战役中，伯利恒主教所携的十字架奇迹般地伸展，高达天际，两臂横贯整个地平线。异教徒看见，便惊惶溃逃。霍维登 (Hoveden)，II. 133及下页。↩︎

  23. 黑格尔 (Hegel)：《历史哲学》(Philosophie der Gesch.)，第3版，1848年，第476页，极为有力地阐明了这一观念。↩︎

  24. 勒里希特 (Röhricht)：《第一次十字军东征史》(Gesch. d. ersten Kreuzzuges)，第6页，说在这些斗争中，“十字军的热忱诞生了”。↩︎

  25. 见格列高利 (Gregory) 那段优美的见证；他曾劝一位卡帕多西亚 (Cappadocia) 的修院院长，不要带领修士前往耶路撒冷。沙弗 (Schaff)：《教会史》(Ch. Hist.)，III. 906。↩︎

  26. 安茹伯爵黑衣富尔克 (Fulke the Black，987—1040年) 曾三赴耶路撒冷，为亵渎圣物及其他罪行补赎。他曾将年轻的妻子打扮得花枝招展，绑在火刑柱上烧死，又使自己的儿子套上驴的挽具，伏在他脚前。在耶路撒冷，他颈上套着绳索四处行走，以示虔诚。他用牙齿从主的墓石上咬下一块，并将一些至为神圣、价值无可估量的物品带回欧洲，例如使徒的手指，以及曾燃起圣火的灯。奥尔良主教奥多尔里克 (Odolric of Orleans) 花了一磅黄金购得这盏灯，将它悬挂在奥尔良的教堂里；灯的神效在那里治愈了许多病人。↩︎

  27. 豪克 (Hauck)，IV. 79。↩︎

  28. 埃克哈德 (Ekkehard)，5，Rec.，V. 14，说希腊皇帝极为频繁地向西方派遣使节、送出书信，这也许有所夸大：per legationes frequentissimas et epistolas etiam a nobis visas ... lugubriter inclamanter（借着极其频繁的使团，以及连我们也曾见过的书信……悲切地呼求），等等。阿历克修斯 (Alexius) 于1088年致佛兰德的罗伯特 (Robert of Flanders) 的信，曾引起许多考证。哈根迈尔 (Hagenmeyer) 经过极其审慎的研究，断定它是真实的，见《书信集》(Epistulae) 等，10—44。↩︎

  29. 迪尔 (Diehl) 在《十字军东征论集》(Essays on the Crusades)，92中，似乎甚至否认拜占庭皇帝阿历克修斯 (Alexius) 曾向西方求援，并称此事是后人的杜撰。除非要无情地抛弃所有同时代作者的见证，否则实在没有比这种批评更无根据的了。↩︎

  30. 《书信登记册》(Reg.)，I. 49；II. 37；米涅 (Migne)，148，329、390。↩︎

  31. multa millia Christianorum quasi pecudes occidisse（将成千上万的基督徒如牲畜一般杀死），《书信登记册》(Reg.)，I. 49。↩︎

  32. 见朱尔·莱尔 (Jules Lair)：《十、十一世纪若干文本的批判研究：教皇色尔爵四世诏书》等 (Études crit. sur divers textes des Xeet XIesiècles. Bulle du pape Sergius IV.)，巴黎，1899年。莱尔反对里昂 (Riant)、普弗卢克-哈通 (Pflugk-Harttung) 等人的看法，提出理由，主张承认1857年发现的色尔爵书信为真。关于西尔维斯特 (Sylvester) 的书信，见阿韦 (Havet)：《热尔贝书信集》(Lettres de Gerbert)，巴黎，1889年。勒里希特 (Röhricht) 在《第一次十字军东征史》(Gesch. d. ersten Kreuzzuges)，8中，断定西尔维斯特的书信是1095年的伪作。莱尔则试图证明，此信出自色尔爵四世之手。↩︎

  33. 朝圣的日期没有记载，但可以认为是在1092—1094年间。从最早的《法兰克人事迹》(Gesta Francorum) 开始，所有记载都称彼得 (Peter) 为“隐士”。亚琛的阿尔伯特 (Albert of Aachen) 明言他来自亚眠 (Amiens)，没有充分理由怀疑这一点。推罗的威廉 (William of Tyre) 则说他来自“亚眠主教区”。哈根迈尔 (Hagenmeyer)，第39页，认为后一说法符合事实。↩︎

  34. 推罗的威廉 (William of Tyre)，第I卷12，Rec.，I. 35，只用几行文字叙述那些异象及主所说的话。他对彼得会见乌尔班一事的记述同样朴实，也几乎同样简短。他写道，彼得“在罗马附近找到了教皇乌尔班大人，呈上耶路撒冷牧首与基督徒的书信，陈明他们的苦难，以及不洁的族类在圣地所行的可憎之事。他就这样审慎而忠实地完成了所受的托付”。↩︎

  35. 在克莱蒙会议 (Council of Clermont) 上，乌尔班提到有关耶路撒冷荒凉景况的“极多报告”，富尔彻 (Fulcher)，Rec.，III. 324。修士罗伯特 (Robert the Monk)，I. 1，Rec.，III. 727，说：relatio gravis saepissime jam ad aures nostras pervenit（悲惨的报告已屡屡传入我们耳中）。据鲍德里克 (Baldric) 记载，他呼吁听众中许多亲身经历过圣地荒凉景况的人出来作证，Rec.，IV. 14。见哈根迈尔 (Hagenmeyer)，74—77。↩︎

  36. 推罗的威廉 (William of Tyre)，第I卷13，如此记载。后来的作者将彼得的旅程渲染得过于广远。↩︎

  37. 推罗的威廉 (William of Tyre) 没有提到这个使团。原因也许是他对阿历克修斯评价很低，称其诡计多端、背信弃义（II. 5）。↩︎

  38. 没有记载表明，这次会议曾正式颁令发动十字军东征。关于会议的议决，我们只能依靠编年史家零散的陈述，以及另外几份非官方文献。↩︎

  39. 兰克 (Ranke)：《世界史》(Weltgeschichte)。据推罗的威廉 (William of Tyre) 说，隐士彼得曾出席克莱蒙会议。同时代的作者没有提到他在场。↩︎

  40. 格雷戈罗维乌斯 (Gregorovius)，IV. 287，说得不错：“乌尔班这篇演说在世界历史上的重要性，超过了德摩斯梯尼 (Demosthenes) 和西塞罗 (Cicero) 的演说。”↩︎

  41. 修士罗伯特 (Robert the Monk)，I. 1，Rec.，III. 727。记述乌尔班演说的同时代作者有鲍德里克 (Baldric)、吉贝尔 (Guibert)、富尔彻 (Fulcher) 和修士罗伯特。他们都曾出席克莱蒙会议。推罗的威廉 (William of Tyre) 对这篇演说大加铺陈；勒里希特 (Röhricht) 称威廉的记述为一种虚构，却又是此类作品中的杰作——eine Erdichtung die ein Meisterstück seiner Art，等等，见《第一次十字军东征史》(Gesch. des ersten Kreuzzuges)，第20页。勒里希特，第235–239页，以及芒罗 (Munro)，《美国历史评论》(Am. Hist. Rev.)，1906年，第231–243页，都曾尝试重构乌尔班的演说，颇为有趣。这些不同记述不应视为彼此矛盾，而应视为互相补充。勒里希特在第20页表示，没有一篇关于这次演说的记述是“准确的”。不过，演说的精神和基本内容无疑保存了下来。乌尔班着重讲述了东方的求援、耶路撒冷的荒凉和东方基督徒的苦难。见芒罗。↩︎

  42. 富尔彻 (Fulcher)，Rec.，III. 324。我主要依据富尔彻和罗伯特的记述。罗伯特说，求援来自耶路撒冷和君士坦丁堡。↩︎

  43. 修士罗伯特 (Robert the Monk)，I. 2，Rec.，III. 729。罗伯特使用的“大地之脐”一语，umbilicus terrarum，是对耶路撒冷的一种常见称呼。↩︎

  44. 鲍德里克 (Baldric)，Rec.，IV. 15，via brevis est, labor permodicus est qui tamen immarcescibilem vobis rependet coronam，意为“路途短暂，劳苦甚微，却将为你们换来不朽的冠冕”。格列高利七世 (Gregory VII.)，Reg.，II. 37，米涅 (Migne)，148，390，也曾引用《哥林多后书》4:17，作出同样的应许：十字军战士将以片刻的劳苦获得永恒的奖赏。↩︎

  45. Deus vult, Deos lo volt, Diex el volt（上帝如此愿意）。这是记载中这一回应的几种不同形式。关于这一回应的拉丁文形式，我们最早的史料依据是修士罗伯特 (Robert the Monk)，I. 2，Rec.，III. 729。他说，众人齐声高呼，una vociferatio：“Deus vult, Deus vult”（上帝如此愿意，上帝如此愿意）。↩︎

  46. 第一次十字军东征中，所有十字标记都是红色的。后来才开始使用绿色和白色。乌尔班亲自分发十字标记。吉贝尔 (Guibert)，II. 5，Rec.，IV. 140，以及富尔彻 (Fulcher)，I. 4，都说乌尔班让十字军战士佩戴十字作为徽记。↩︎

  47. 乌尔班在克莱蒙演说之后为推进此事所写的信，载于哈根迈耶 (Hagenmeyer)，《书信集》(Epistulae)，第136页及后。↩︎

  48. Petrum more heremi vilissima cappa tegebat，“彼得依隐修者的习惯，披着一件极粗陋的斗篷”，卡昂的拉杜尔夫 (Radulf of Caen)。上述描写取自严格意义上的同时代记述。↩︎

  49. 拉杜尔夫 (Radulf) 所说的 statura brevis（身材矮小），到了推罗的威廉 (William of Tyre) 的记述中，就成了 pusillus, persona contemptibilis（矮小，形貌卑微）。↩︎

  50. 我如此翻译了拉杜尔夫 (Radulf) 的 spiritus acer（精神锐烈）一语。↩︎

  51. 亚琛的阿尔伯特 (Albert of Aachen)：neminem invenerunt qui tam ferocissimo et superbo loqui auderet quousque Petrus. 意为：“在彼得之前，他们找不到一个敢向这般凶暴而傲慢的人说话的人。”↩︎

  52. 因此，吉贝尔 (Guibert) 称那些听他讲说的人群为 tanta populorum multitudo（如此众多的民众）。哈根迈耶 (Hagenmeyer)，第114页，接受吉贝尔的说法，称之为极庞大的人群，ungeheure Zahl。↩︎

  53. 吉贝尔 (Guibert)：quidquid agebat namque seu loquebatur quasi quiddam subdivinum videbatur，意为：“他无论做什么、说什么，似乎都带着某种近乎神圣的意味。”↩︎

  54. 埃克哈德 (Ekkehard)，XII.，Rec.，V. 20及下页，也这样说。他对所有这些传道者以及彼得后来的生涯，都有一些贬抑之词。Quem postea multi hypocritam esse dicebant，意为：“后来许多人说他是个伪善者。”↩︎

  55. 修士罗伯特 (Robert the Monk)，I. 5，Rec.，III. 731。Super ipsos praesules et abbates apice religionis efferebatur，意为：“他在虔敬上的崇高声望，甚至凌驾于主教和修道院院长之上。”↩︎

  56. 吉贝尔 (Guibert)：neminem meminerim similem honore haberi，意为：“我不记得有谁曾受到同样的尊崇。”鲍德里克 (Baldric) 称他为 Petrus quidam magnus heremita，用我们的话来说，就是“那位伟大的隐士彼得”。↩︎

  57. 黑格尔 (Hegel)，《历史哲学》(Philosophie der Gesch.)，第444页，称十字军东征为“中世纪的顶峰”。像诺让的吉贝尔 (Guibert of Nogent)，123，这样的同时代人，想不出还有哪一场运动在荣耀上可与十字军东征相比。奥德里克·维塔利斯 (Ordericus Vitalis)，III. 458，赞扬不同语言的民族联合起来，投身于这样一项值得称颂的事业。↩︎

  58. 关于这些早期远征的记述，我们主要依靠亚琛的阿尔伯特 (Albert of Aachen)。吉贝尔 (Guibert) 没有区分各支队伍，对彼得到达君士坦丁堡之前的远征，也只作了简略记载。↩︎

  59. Sine Pecunia、Sansavoir、Habenichts，均有“一无所有”之意。勒里希特 (Röhricht) 及其他历史学家将这些先期远征称为 Die Züge der Bauern，即农民的远征。↩︎

  60. 见哈根迈耶 (Hagenmeyer)，204及下页。彼得为战败向皇帝致歉，理由是自己无法约束追随者；他宣称，这些人不配见到耶路撒冷。安娜·科穆宁娜 (Anna Comnena) 称彼得为“自高自大的拉丁人”。↩︎

  61. I. 26。↩︎

  62. 安娜·科穆宁娜 (Anna Comnena) 说，十字军从四面八方如河流般汇聚而来。她说彼得的军队有步兵八万、骑兵十万。富尔彻 (Fulcher) 说，从西方出发的人数是“一支庞大的队伍。海中的众岛和全地都受上帝感动，为这支大军贡献力量。留下的人悲伤，出发的人欢喜”。↩︎

  63. 这是根据亚琛的阿尔伯特 (Albert of Aachen) 和吉贝尔 (Guibert) 的见证。见勒里希特 (Röhricht)，《第一次十字军东征》(Erster Kreuzzug)，240及下页，以及该处所列参考文献。↩︎

  64. 曼海默 (Mannheimer)，《1096年第一次十字军东征期间施派尔、沃尔姆斯和美因茨的犹太人迫害》(Die Judenverfolgungen in Speier, Worms und Mainz im Jahre 1096, während des ersten Kreuzzuges)，达姆施塔特 (Darmstadt)，1877年。哈根迈耶 (Hagenmeyer)，第139页，洗去了亚眠的彼得 (Peter of Amiens) 发起这场种族屠杀的可耻“荣誉”，并恰当地将它归给埃米希伯爵 (count Emich) 及其暴民。另见勒里希特 (Röhricht)，《第一次十字军东征史》(Gesch. d. ersten Kreuzzuges)，41–46。↩︎

  65. 亚琛的阿尔伯特 (Albert of Aachen)，II. 18。↩︎

  66. 吉本 (Gibbon) 称他为“一位可敬的高级教士，无论对今世还是来世之事，都同样胜任”。↩︎

  67. 布永 (Bouillon)，不可与英吉利海峡沿岸的滨海布洛涅 (Boulogne-sur-mer) 混淆，是比利时卢森堡 (Luxemburg) 的一座城镇，原为布永领地的首府。戈弗雷 (Godfrey) 于1095年将这片领地抵押给列日 (Liège) 主教。自1831年以来，该地属于比利时。↩︎

  68. 吉本 (Gibbon)：“在坦克雷德 (Tancred) 完美的品格中，我们看到了一个完美骑士的一切美德，看到了真正的骑士精神；这种精神比当时卑劣的哲学或更卑劣的宗教，更能激发人慷慨的情怀，并促使人履行社会义务。”↩︎

  69. 富尔彻 (Fulcher)，I. 13，Rec.，III. 336。↩︎

  70. 阿吉莱的雷蒙 (Raymund of Agiles) 说，阿历克修斯 (Alexius) 如此对待十字军大军，以致“只要他还活着，人民就会咒诅他，称他为叛徒”。↩︎

  71. 同时代史料记载，受到斥责的是卡尔彭塔里乌斯 (Carpentarius)。正如哈根迈耶 (Hagenmeyer) 所指出的，彼得也在受责者之列，只因卡尔彭塔里乌斯具有王族血统，才单独提到他的名字。↩︎

  72. 阿吉莱的雷蒙 (Raymund of Agiles) 也曾参与挖掘这件兵器。它的真伪引起争论，阿德马尔 (Adhemar) 也是怀疑者之一。巴泰勒米 (Barthelemy) 为证明自己所言属实，接受了火刑神判，却因所受的伤而死。↩︎

  73. 据修士罗伯特 (Robert the Monk)，IV.，Rec.，III. 824，最后进攻前夕，天上显了一个兆头：西方天空燃起一道火焰，ignis de coelo veniens ab occidente（从西方天际降下的火）。十字军时代遗留下来的有趣文献中，有沙特尔伯爵斯蒂芬 (Stephen, count of Chartres) 写给妻子阿黛勒 (Adele) 的两封信，一封写于尼西亚 (Nicaea) 城下，另一封写于围攻安提阿 (Antioch) 期间。两信载于哈根迈耶 (Hagenmeyer)，《书信集》(Epistulae)，第138、149页。↩︎

  74. 关于人数，各种记载不一。见叙贝尔 (Sybel)，412，以及勒里希特 (Röhricht)，《第一次十字军东征史》(Gesch. des ersten Kreuzzuges)，183。推罗的威廉 (William of Tyre) 给出的数字是二万一千人，而耶路撒冷守军则为四万人。↩︎

  75. 阿吉莱的雷蒙 (Raymund of Agiles) 记载，十字军忘记了彼得·巴泰勒米 (Peter Barthelemy) 关于赤脚走完最后一段路程的劝勉。“他们不再记得自己的疲惫，加快脚步，在眼泪与赞美中抵达城墙。”↩︎

  76. 这一次，隐士彼得 (Peter the Hermit) 和后来成为耶路撒冷大主教的阿尔努尔夫 (Arnulf)，在橄榄山发表演说，为要恢复十字军领袖间的团结，尤其是坦克雷德 (Tancred) 与雷蒙 (Raymund) 之间的团结。亚琛的阿尔伯特 (Albert of Aachen)，VI. 8，Rec.，IV. 471，说：ad populos sermones ... plurimam discordiam quae inter Peregrinos de diversis causis excreverat exstinxerunt，意为：“向众人所作的演说……平息了朝圣者之间因种种缘故而滋长的大量纷争。”坦克雷德在伯利恒升起自己的旗帜，曾引起许多嫉妒。哈根迈耶 (Hagenmeyer)，第259页，反对叙贝尔 (Sybel) 的看法，认为阿尔伯特的记述是真实的。↩︎

  77. Miles splendidus et refulgens. “一位光辉灿烂的战士。”↩︎

  78. 吉贝尔 (Guibert)，VII. 7，Rec.，IV. 226；修士罗伯特 (Robert the Monk)，VII.，Rec.，III. 867。↩︎

  79. 目击者阿吉莱的雷蒙 (Raymund of Agiles) 如此说：usque ad genua et usque ad frenos equorum（直至膝盖，直至马的嚼环），XX.，Rec，III. 300。他称此为“上帝公义的审判”。↩︎

  80. 《事迹》(Gesta) 如此说：tales occisiones de paganorum gente nullus unquam audivit nec vidit ... nemo scit numerum eorum nisi solus deus，意为：“对异教徒如此惨烈的屠杀，向来无人听见，也无人见过……除上帝以外，无人知道他们的数目。”关于死者人数，估计从四万到十万不等。吉贝尔 (Guibert)，《事迹》(Gesta)，VII. 7，Rec.，IV. 227，进一步说，圣殿区域血流成海，sanguinis unda（血浪），几乎要淹没步行的人。↩︎

  81. IX.，Rec.，III. 869。罗伯特描绘了一幅可怖的景象：街道上堆满残肢断体，鲜血横流。↩︎

  82. 推罗的威廉 (William of Tyre) 是这一场景最早的记述者。除去那些渲染之处，这一场景似乎并无任何不合情理之处。哈根迈耶 (Hagenmeyer)，第265–269页，称其为“纯属威廉凭想象虚构”。↩︎

  83. 叙贝尔 (Sybel)，《第一次十字军东征史》(Gesch. des ersten Kreuzzugs)，第ii页。↩︎

  84. 哈根迈耶 (Hagenmeyer)，《隐士彼得》(Peter der Eremite)，第102页，说：Dem Papste allein ist der Ruhm zu erhalten den ihm der Einsiedler von Amiens bis auf unsere Tage zur grösseren Hälfte streitig gemacht hat. 意为：“这份荣耀应当只归给教皇；直到我们今日，亚眠的隐士仍在与他争夺其中的大半。”另见叙贝尔 (Sybel)，第243页。↩︎

  87. 他说，他所记述的是自己听闻的事，ex auditu et relatione（根据耳闻与转述）。↩︎

  88. Nach einer solchen Katastrophe war ofenbar auch bei diesen alles Ansehen für ihn dabei，意为：“在这样一场灾难之后，显然连这些人也不再尊重他了。”哈根迈耶 (Hagenmeyer)，第204页。↩︎

  89. Ut stellae quoque juxta Apocalypsim de coelo cadere viderentur, Petrus ille，等等，意为：“仿佛连众星也照着《启示录》所说的从天上坠落，那位彼得……”。↩︎

  90. 埃克哈德 (Ekkehard)，XIII.，Rec.，V. 21，说彼得的部众一到小亚细亚，就成了土耳其人嘲笑的对象，cohortes ...paganis fuerant jam ludibrio factae（这些部众……已经成为异教徒的笑柄）。↩︎

  91. 哈根迈耶 (Hagenmeyer)，第220页及后、243页，认为到威廉写作之时，这类事情已不再有人传述。↩︎

  92. 这些国王的正式头衔是 rex Latinorum in Hierusalem（耶路撒冷的拉丁人之王）。推罗的威廉 (William of Tyre) 谈到戈弗雷 (Godfrey) 拒绝王冠时说：“戈弗雷如此行，是出于一位有信仰的君主的心志。他是最好的君王，是所有其他君王的明灯与镜鉴”，lumen et speculum，IX. 9，Rec.，I. 377。教士们曾梦想使耶路撒冷的世俗政府完全隶属于牧首领导下的属灵政府。第一任牧首不仅取得耶路撒冷和雅法 (Jaffa) 四分之一地域的管辖权，还得到戈弗雷的承诺：倘若戈弗雷成功攻取开罗 (Cairo) 或另一座敌方大城，或去世时没有男性继承人，便将这两座城市全部交给牧首。见勒里希特 (Röhricht)，《第一次十字军东征史》(Gesch. des ersten Kreuzzuges)，第218页。↩︎

  93. 见达戈贝尔特 (Dagobert) 的呼吁书，载哈根迈耶 (Hagenmeyer)，《书信集》(Epistulae)，176及下页、412及后。他称“耶路撒冷的圣洁超乎一切地方”，并说，“正因如此，它才受到异教徒和不信者的压迫”。富尔彻 (Fulcher) 记述1100年之事时宣称，留下来保卫耶路撒冷、雅法 (Jaffa) 和拉姆拉 (Ramleh) 的，只有三百名骑士和同样数目的步兵。引文见哈根迈耶，415。↩︎

  94. Hic jacet inclitus dux Godefridus de Bouillon qui totam sitam terram acquisivit cultui christiano, cujus anima regnet cum Christo. “这里安息着声名显赫的布永公爵戈弗雷 (Godefridus de Bouillon)，他取得了这整片土地，使之归于基督教的敬拜；愿他的灵魂与基督一同作王。”↩︎

  95. 据阿吉莱的雷蒙 (Raymund of Agiles) 说，阿尔努尔夫 (Arnulf) 生活放荡，他的风流韵事成了军营歌曲的题材。↩︎

  96. 从1291年阿克 (Acre) 陷落至1848年，除两人外，历任牧首都住在罗马。1848年，由庇护九世 (Pius IX.) 任命为牧首的瓦莱尔加 (Valerga) 定居耶路撒冷。↩︎

  97. 维尔肯 (Wilken) 对这一题目作了长篇论述，I. 第307–424页。↩︎

  98. Fulani，“随便什么人”。fulan ibn fulan 这一称呼，意为“某某，某某之子”，在阿拉伯人中是一种极具侮辱性的称呼方式。↩︎

  99. 以下制服一个强盗部族的手段颇能显示其作风。强盗躲进一处洞穴，鲍德温 (Baldwin) 便用烟熏，迫使他们出来。两人走出后，鲍德温温言相待，给其中一人穿上衣服，许下好话，遣他回去，却将另一人处死。随后又有十人出来，其中一人被遣回，其余九人被处死。他继续采用同样的手段，直到诱出二百三十人，将他们处死。随后又重新点火，直到所有人都出来，遭受同样的命运。↩︎

  100. 从这里起，推罗的威廉 (William of Tyre) 以目击者的身份记述，见第XVI卷及后各卷。↩︎

  101. 据圣殿骑士团团长特里基乌斯 (Terricius) 的信，有二百九十名圣殿骑士丧生；撒拉森人 (Saracens) 如蚁群般遍布从推罗 (Tyre) 到加沙 (Gaza) 的全境。霍夫登的理查 (Richard of Hoveden) 在1187年条下（an. 1187）说，圣殿骑士奋战如狮。↩︎

  102. 萨拉丁 (Saladin) 给居伊 (Guy) 一杯水。居伊将水递给雷金纳德 (Reginald) 时，萨拉丁喊道：“我没有吩咐这样做。是你给他的。”随即亲手或命仆人杀死了雷金纳德。雷金纳德曾抢劫一支商队，萨拉丁的妹妹当时就在其中。莱恩-普尔 (Lane-Poole)，《萨拉丁》(Saladin)，第215页。↩︎

  103. 一个拜占金币 (bezant) 值三美元。↩︎

  104. 见弗赖辛的奥托 (Otto of Freising)，VII. 30。↩︎

  105. 戈特洛布 (Gottlob)，《十字军赎罪券》(Kreuzablass)，第106页及后。尤金尼乌斯 (Eugenius) 引用了乌尔班二世 (Urban II) 在克莱蒙 (Clermont) 颁布的赎罪诏令。↩︎

  106. 《论省察》(De consideratione)，II. 1，赖因肯斯 (Reinkens) 译本，第31–37页。在这部著名论著的这一章中，伯纳德 (Bernard) 解释了自己在十字军运动中的作为，并为之辩护。↩︎

  107. 奥多 (Odo)，I. 1，caeperunt undique conclamando cruces expetere ... coactus est vestes suas in cruces scindere et seminare：“他们开始从四面八方高声呼求十字架……他不得不把自己的衣服撕成十字架，分发出去。”↩︎

  108. 作为康拉德 (Konrad) 力量过人的证明，推罗的威廉 (William of Tyre)，XVII. 4，记述说，围攻大马士革 (Damascus) 时，他一刀劈下一名身穿铠甲的人，从头、颈、肩一直砍到腋下。↩︎

  109. 第XVI卷，第20章。威廉 (William) 暗示，曼努埃尔 (Manuel) 心生嫉妒，是因为康拉德 (Konrad) 自称罗马人的国王。迪尔 (Diehl)，《十字军论集》(Essays on the Crusades)，第107页，对曼努埃尔的向导蓄意误导并出卖德意志人的说法表示怀疑。不过，他承认，小亚细亚的希腊居民“敲诈拉丁人，或使他们挨饿”。↩︎

  110. 推罗的威廉 (William of Tyre)，第XVII卷，列出了在场显要人物的名单，皇帝的兄弟、弗赖辛主教奥托 (Otto of Freising) 也在其中。↩︎

  111. 《论省察》(De consideratione)，II. 1。↩︎

  112. 理查 (Richard) 抓住一头狮子，挖出其仍在跳动的心脏的故事，是十四世纪英国传奇的题材，很可能也是十三世纪法国传奇的题材。↩︎

  113. 船只从阿克 (Acre) 驶往马赛 (Marseilles)，至少需要十五天；消息从耶路撒冷传到罗马，也大约需要同样长的时间。1181年，亚历山大三世 (Alexander III.) 得知萨拉丁 (Saladin) 征服埃及，并在巴尼亚斯 (Banias) 击败基督徒的消息后，向十字军战士许诺了赎罪券；戈特洛布 (Gottlob)，第119页及下页，引用了这些赎罪条款。亚历山大援引了乌尔班二世 (Urban II) 和尤金尼乌斯三世 (Eugenius III.) 的先例。↩︎

  114. 他以3,000英镑卖出了约克 (York) 大主教的职位。据说亨利 (Henry) 留下了价值900,000英镑的金银。温多弗的罗杰 (Rog. of Wendover)，1180年条（an. 1180）。↩︎

  115. 迪韦齐斯的理查 (Richard of Devizes)，X。↩︎

  116. 威尔士的杰拉尔德 (Giraldus Cambrensis) 陪同大主教前往，并沿途为他的游记搜集材料。↩︎

  117. 腓特烈 (Frederick) 在致萨拉丁 (Saladin) 的一封信中宣布了自己的远征计划，并在信中逐一列举将参加远征的各个部族，从“高大的巴伐利亚人”到威尼斯 (Venice) 和比萨 (Pisa) 的水手。见 Itin. reg. Ricardi de Hoveden 等。↩︎

  118. 兰克 (Ranke)，VIII. 第246页及后，饶有兴味地推想伊萨克 (Isaac) 被废黜可能带来的后果；作为一个德意志人，他对腓特烈 (Frederick) 没有夺取这一战利品感到遗憾：Es war ein Moment das nicht so leicht wieder kommen konnte，“这样的时机不易再来。”↩︎

  119. 另一位随军者的记述则说，巴巴罗萨 (Barbarossa) 急于前进，试图游过河去，结果溺死。兰克 (Ranke)，VIII. 第249页，认为正文所采用的说法更为可信。↩︎

  120. 《行记》(Itinerary)，III. 16。↩︎

  121. 理查 (Richard) 从墨西拿 (Messina) 启航时，舰队由一百五十艘大船和五十三艘桨帆船组成。↩︎

  122. 《行记》(Itinerary)，III. 2，说理查 (Richard) 抵达时，众人欣喜若狂，欢呼吹号，迎接他的到来。他被迎上岸，仿佛万国所羡慕的已经来到；蜡炬与火光照彻黑夜，“仿佛白昼的光辉侵占了黑夜，土耳其人以为整个山谷都着了火”。迪韦齐斯的理查 (Richard of Devizes)，LXIII，说：“围城者迎接理查时的喜悦，仿佛基督再次降临一般。”↩︎

  123. 《行记》(Itinerary)，I. 66，说鲍德温 (Baldwin) 看到“全军放荡不羁，沉溺于饮酒、女色和掷骰赌博”，便病倒了，以至于死。↩︎

  124. 阿克 (Acre) 城下的损失极其惨重。《行记》(Itinerary) 列出阵亡者，有6位大主教、12位主教、40位伯爵和500名骑士，见IV. 6。霍夫登 (De Hoveden) 也列出了一份惊人的名单，其中包括施瓦本 (Swabia)、佛兰德 (Flanders) 和勃艮第 (Burgundy) 的公爵，以及贝桑松 (Besançon)、阿尔勒 (Arles)、蒙特雷阿尔 (Montreal) 等地的大主教。鲍德温 (Baldwin) 于1190年11月19日去世。《行记》把阿克之围比作特洛伊 (Troy) 之围，并说（I. 32）：“作为一座全世界为之争夺的城市，它必将赢得永恒的声名。”↩︎

  125. 《行记》(Itinerary) 及其他文献经常提到它的致命用途。双方使用的器械包括投石机 petrariae，以及用来投掷石块和其他投射物的抛石机 (mangonels)。见《行记》(Itinerary)，III. 7等。其中一种钩攀器械称为“猫”。攻城者还使用撞城槌，以及称为“母猪”(sow) 的掩蔽物，在其遮护下接近城墙。理查王 (King Richard) 精于使用 arbalest，即弩。↩︎

  126. 一条面包的价格从一便士涨到40先令，一匹马所驮的谷物卖到60马克。见霍夫登 (De Hoveden) 等。人们贪婪地吞食马肉，连马肠也不放过，马肠售价达10苏尔 (sols)。甚至连草也成了人们寻求的充饥之物。《行记》(Itinerary)，I. 67–83，生动描述了饥荒造成的悲惨苦难。↩︎

  127. 《行记》(Itinerary)，I. 44。↩︎

  128. 这一借口的唯一依据是霍夫登 (de Hoveden) 在1191年条（an. 1191）中的记述。然而，他说，萨拉丁 (Saladin) 是在理查 (Richard) 拒绝收回威胁，也不肯宽限缴纳赎金和交还真十字架的期限之后，才处死基督徒俘虏的。阿彻 (Archer)，《十字军史》(Hist. of the Crusades)，第331页，认为巴巴丁 (Baba-ed-din) 的记述暗示萨拉丁实施了屠杀；但莱恩-普尔 (Lane-Poole)，《萨拉丁传》(Life of Saladin)，第307页，持相反意见。《行记》(Itinerary)，IV. 4，记述理查的部下“跃身上前执行他的命令，感谢神的恩典，准许他们这样为那些被俘虏们用弩箭和弓箭杀死的基督徒报仇”。它完全没有提到萨拉丁进行过屠杀。莱恩-普尔出于对萨拉丁的倾慕而失之偏颇，借此说道：“在十字军的争战中，文明、宽宏、宽容、真正的骑士精神和温雅修养等美德，全都在撒拉森人 (Saracens) 一边。”勃艮第公爵 (duke of Burgundy) 也参与了对土耳其俘虏的屠杀。↩︎

  129. 《行记》(Itinerary)，VI. 23。理查 (Richard) 经常参加混战，《行记》(Itinerary)，VI. 4，对其中一次有如下描述：“理查以其王者风度，在众人之中格外显眼。他骑着一匹高大的战马，单骑冲向敌军。接连的击打使他的白蜡木长矛碎裂；但他立即拔剑，紧追逃窜的土耳其人，将他们如割草般砍倒，扫除落后者，制服在前者。他就这样雷霆般向前冲杀，劈砍不休。任何铠甲都挡不住他的击打，因为他的剑锋将人的头从顶端直劈至牙齿。他如此来回挥剑，吓得溃败的土耳其人四散奔逃，仿佛狼追赶逃窜的羊群。”↩︎

  130. 德·茹安维尔 (De Joinville)，《圣路易传》(Life of St. Louis)，1253年条（an. 1253），说若不是勃艮第公爵 (Duke of Burgundy) 的嫉妒与背叛，理查 (Richard) 本来就会攻取耶路撒冷，这无疑是实情。他复述了理查的一句话，精妙得几乎不可能不是真的。一位军官说：“陛下，请到这里来，我指给您看耶路撒冷。”国王放下兵器，举目望天，喊道：“主上帝啊，我祈求你，在我能把圣城从你的仇敌手中解救出来之前，永不让我看见它。”《行记》(Itinerary) 对此只字未提。迪韦齐斯的理查 (Richard of Devizes)，XC，记述说，索尔兹伯里主教休伯特 (Hubert, bishop of Salisbury) 在耶路撒冷朝圣后，劝国王以朝圣者的身份入城，但“他高贵心灵中义愤难平，不肯因异教徒的礼遇而接受他未能靠上帝的恩赐获得的东西”。↩︎

  131. 巴哈丁 (Baha-ed-din)，引自莱恩-普尔 (Lane-Poole)，第354页。霍夫登 (De Hoveden) 说的是水果，《行记》(Itinerary) 说的是马匹。后来的传说称，萨拉丁 (Saladin) 每年赐给阿克 (Acre) 的圣约翰骑士团 (Knights of St. John) 一千拜占金币 (bezants)。为了检验骑士们的仁爱，苏丹曾乔装前往医院，发现有关他们仁慈待人的传闻确实不虚。关于这件事，以及他从托隆的汉弗莱 (Humphrey of Toron) 手中受封骑士的故事——后者有同时代的《理查王行记》(Itinerary of King Richard) 为证——阿拉伯史料全无记载。见莱恩-普尔，《萨拉丁传》(Life of Saladin)，第387页及后。↩︎

  132. 博韦的文森特 (Vincent de Beauvais) 记载了一则西方传说：萨拉丁 (Saladin) 临终时，将掌旗官叫到跟前，命他像从前高举出征战旗那样，举着他死亡的旗帜，走遍大马士革 (Damascus) 的街道。这旗帜就是系在长矛上的一块破布，掌旗官要高喊：“看哪，东方之王死时，除这块布以外，什么也带不走。”↩︎

  133. 《行记》(Itinerary) 讲述了一个关于萨拉丁 (Saladin) 和那臭名昭著的圣火奇迹的故事；直到不久以前，圣墓教堂 (church of the Holy Sepulchre) 还在展示这种奇迹。这个故事很可能是真的。有一次，萨拉丁看见圣火降下，点燃了一盏灯，便命人把灯吹灭，以证明这是一场骗局。但灯立刻又燃了起来，仿佛出于神迹。第二次、第三次熄灭后，它仍一再复燃。“啊，抗拒那看不见的大能，有什么用呢！”《行记》的作者如此感叹，见V. 16。↩︎

  134. 胡尔特 (Hurter) 认为，流传下来的数字大为夸张。↩︎

  135. 一首讽刺诗这样嘲讽这场运动的愚妄：——

    “Ad mare stultorum

    Tendebat iter puerorum。”

    “通往愚人之海的，

    是孩子们走的路。”↩︎

  136. 维尔肯 (Wilken) 为这一说法引用了彼得·比扎里 (Peter Bizari) 的《热那亚元老院与人民史》(History of the Genoese Senate and People)，安特卫普 (Antwerp)，1679年。其中一个家族是维瓦尔第家族 (Vivaldi)。↩︎

  137. 见维尔肯 (Wilken)，VI. 第83页。↩︎

  138. 维尔肯 (Wilken) 如此说：Sie ist durch die Zeugnisse glaubwürdiger Geschichtschreiber so fest begründet, dass ihre Wahrheit nicht bezweifelt werden kann，“它有可信的历史学家的证言作为如此坚实的依据，其真实性不容怀疑”，第72页。勒里希特 (Röhricht)，《历史杂志》(Hist. Zeitschrift)，XXXVI. 5，也坚持这些记述在历史上的真实性。↩︎

  139. 见胡尔特 (Hurter)，I. 第221–230页等处的详尽描述。↩︎

  140. 《英诺森书信集》(Epp. of Innocent)，I. 353、354等，米涅 (Migne)，214，第329页及后。↩︎

  141. 《书信》(Ep.)，I. 353，米涅 (Migne)，214，第325页及后。↩︎

  142. 贡特鲁斯 (Guntherus)，米涅 (Migne)，212，第225页。↩︎

  143. 沙拉松 (Charasson)，第99页，载有英诺森 (Innocent) 委派富尔克 (Fulke) 宣讲十字军运动的信的法文译本。↩︎

  144. 蒂博 (Thibaut) 当时二十二岁，路易 (Louis) 二十七岁，都是法国国王的侄子，见维尔阿杜安 (Villehardouin)，3；瓦伊 (Wailly) 编本，第5页。十字军尚未从法国出发，蒂博就已去世。↩︎

  145. 维尔阿杜安 (Villehardouin) 是六人委员会的成员之一（瓦伊 [Wailly] 编本，第11页），他说：“在那里消灭土耳其人，比在任何别的国家都容易。”十字军常把埃及称为“巴比伦之地”。↩︎

  146. 瓦伊 (Wailly) 编订的维尔阿杜安 (Villehardouin) 著作，第452页，将这笔款项折算为4,420,000法郎，以一马克合52法郎计算。大议会“为爱上帝之故”，另加五十艘武装桨帆船，条件是在同盟存续期间，威尼斯 (Venice) 应获得征服所得战利品的一半。↩︎

  147. 维尔阿杜安 (Villehardouin) 称他为一位 de bien grand coeur（胸怀极为宽广）的人。他于1205年去世，享年九十七岁，葬于圣索菲亚教堂 (Church of St. Sophia)。总督在答复代表团时，以如下话语承认十字军诸领袖出身高贵：“我们看出，诸位大人在不戴王冠的人中，位居最高之列。”（维尔阿杜安，16；瓦伊 [Wailly] 编本，13。）↩︎

  148. 维尔阿杜安 (Villehardouin)，56及后；瓦伊 (Wailly) 编本，33及下页。↩︎

  149. 维尔阿杜安 (Villehardouin) 只提到进攻扎拉 (Zara) 的提议。克拉里的罗贝尔 (Robert of Clary) 及其他作者说，丹多洛 (Dandolo) 先前曾提议，舰队应驶往穆斯林领土，并将最初所得的战利品用于偿还十字军的债务。随后，他以进攻扎拉的提议取而代之，十字军迫于处境，不得不接受。关于丹多洛于1202年5月同埃及苏丹订立秘密条约的指控，并非全无根据。见皮尔斯 (Pears)，271及后。↩︎

  150. 维尔阿杜安 (Villehardouin) 与克拉里的罗贝尔 (Robert de Clary)。克拉里的记述十分生动，在两者之中也详尽得多。↩︎

  151. 后来，一个代表团拜访英诺森 (Innocent)，获得了他的赦免。维尔阿杜安 (Villehardouin)，107；瓦伊 (Wailly) 编本，61。讷伊的富尔克 (Fulke of Neuilly) 去世的消息，在十字军离开威尼斯的前夕传到军中。维尔阿杜安，73；瓦伊编本，43，称他为 le bon, le saint homme（那位良善、圣洁的人）。↩︎

  152. 维尔阿杜安 (Villehardouin)，109。皮尔斯 (Pears)，第268页，以悲怆的笔调说，十字军“即将犯下中世纪的大罪，摧毁那座堡垒；此前势不可当的穆斯林入侵浪潮，曾冲击这座堡垒，却在它面前破碎”。十字军的动机固然不值得称赞，但也没有必要为君士坦丁堡 (Constantinople) 和希腊人的命运哀叹。拉丁人的征服延长了成功抵御土耳其人的时期。↩︎

  153. 阿拉伯人获准居住在城内，并享有举行其宗教仪式的权利。吉本 (Gibbon) 以其特有的讽刺口吻说：“佛兰德的朝圣者看到清真寺或犹太会堂，里面所敬拜的独一上帝既无伴侣，也无儿子，便大为愤慨。”↩︎

  154. 维尔阿杜安 (Villehardouin)，233；瓦伊 (Wailly) 编本，第137页，断言攻取君士坦丁堡是人所尝试过的最艰难的壮举之一，une des plus redoutables choses à faire qui jamais fut。法兰克人 (Franks) 此前从未见过防御工事如此坚固的城市。↩︎

  155. 胡尔特 (Hurter)（I，第685页）将征服君士坦丁堡与攻取耶路撒冷相比，褒扬戈德弗鲁瓦 (Godfrey) 和第一次十字军的虔诚，将他们与贪图战利品的威尼斯人相对照。他忘记了耶路撒冷那场可怕的大屠杀。↩︎

  158. 维尔阿杜安 (Villehardouin)，191；瓦伊 (Wailly) 编本，111，说：des reliques it n’en faut point parler, car en ce jour il y en avait autant dans la ville que dans le reste du monde（至于圣物，更不必说了，因为当时城中的圣物，与世界其余地方的总和一样多）。贡特鲁斯 (Guntherus) 的记述最为详尽，见米涅 (Migne)，212，253及后。他的消息提供者马丁院长 (Abbot Martin)，是一位搜求圣物永不知足的人。↩︎

  165. 皮尔斯 (Pears) 在《君士坦丁堡的陷落》(The Fall of Constantinople) 一书结尾作出错误的判断，认为第四次十字军运动对于文明的影响完全是灾难性的。他推想，倘若没有这次十字军运动，该城就永远不会落入土耳其人之手，而马尔马拉海 (Sea of Marmora) 和黑海如今就会被“繁荣而文明的国家”所环绕，见第412页及后。拜占庭帝国 (Byzantine empire) 当时并没有什么进步运动可供十字军阻碍。↩︎

  168. 芬克 (Funk) 在韦策尔—韦尔特 (Wetzer-Welte)，VII，1166中说，鉴于同时代人的见证，腓特烈 (Frederick) 患病一事不容怀疑。然而，罗杰·温多弗 (Roger Wendover) 在1227年条（an. 1227）中对此表示怀疑。芬克说，直到1239年，格列高利 (Gregory) 因皇帝的行为而愤怒，才质疑腓特烈以患病为由的辩解；这话不对。在1228年的绝罚判词中，格列高利已断言，腓特烈二世“被诱回去享受其王国中惯有的逸乐，并以身体不适为轻率的借口”。1235年，皇帝与教皇和好期间，格列高利曾谈到耶路撒冷，“已归还给我们在基督里亲爱的儿子腓特烈”。↩︎

  176. 茹安维尔 (Joinville) 由二十名骑士陪同，在塞浦路斯 (Cyprus) 与国王会合。他宗教热忱深厚，出发以前曾到城堡附近所有圣所朝拜；在长期离乡期间，每逢星期五，他总是只吃饼、喝水（《历史》[History]，1250年条 [an. 1250]）。他的一段文字生动地揭示了数以千计的十字军将士在离开家园、踏上漫长而前途未卜的东方旅程时，必然曾感受的悲伤。他写道：“经过茹安维尔城堡附近时，我始终不敢转眼望向那里，唯恐在离开我的孩子，以及我心中所爱的美丽的茹安维尔城堡时，感到过于深切的眷恋，以致勇气尽失。”↩︎

  177. 茹安维尔 (Joinville) 说，路易 (Louis)“在和平时期维系自己部众团结所遇到的困难，与他遭逢厄运时一样多”。1249年条（an. 1249）。↩︎

  192. 对于十字架的仇敌，无论多么卑贱的称呼，人们都用得出来。最常见的称呼是狗。理查一世 (Richard I) 的传记作者，上一行还在叙述撒拉森人 (Saracens) 所赠的礼物，下一行便称他们为狗，毫无愧疚。见《理查行纪》(Itin. Ricardi) 等。因此，沃尔特·马普 (Walter Map) 说：sepulchrum et crux Domini praeda sunt canum quorum fames in tantum lassata fuit et sanguine martyrorum，等等（主的坟墓与十字架成了狗的掠物；它们的饥渴竟到了如此地步，连殉道者的血也使其厌倦）。赖特 (Wright) 编本，I，15，第229页。↩︎

  193. 罗马尼斯的洪贝尔 (Humbert de Romanis) 于1274年也如此说；曼西 (Mansi)，XXIV，116。他所列举并答辩的反对十字军运动的第六项异议如下：quod ex ista pugna non sequitur fructus spiritualis quia Saraceni magis convertuntur ad blasphemiam quam ad fidem; occisi autem ad infernum mittuntur，等等（这种战争不产生属灵的果效，因为撒拉森人由此更多地转向亵渎，而不是信仰；被杀的人则被送入地狱）。↩︎

  198. 单是两大军事修会的船只，就运送了大批朝圣者。1182年，它们的一艘船在埃及海岸失事，船上载有1500名朝圣者。1180年，数艘船也遭遇同样的命运，2500名朝圣者溺死，1500人被卖为奴。1246年，它们的船只仅从马赛 (Marseilles) 一港就运走了6000名朝圣者。见普鲁茨 (Prutz)，《论文集》(Essays)，第54页。这位作者在强调十字军运动的经济影响时，恰当地指出，它们“仅在部分程度上与宗教、尤其与教会有关”，第77页。阿拉伯词语，如 damask（锦缎）、tarif（税则）和 bazar（集市），进入了欧洲各民族的词汇；藏红花、玉米、甜瓜和小洋葱等物产，也由十字军带了回来。汇款的需要促成了信用证制度的发展。↩︎

  199. 雄辩的理查德·S. 斯托尔斯博士 (Dr. Richard S. Storrs) 在《克莱尔沃的伯纳德》(Bernard of Clairvaux)，第558页说，十字军运动所展现的“确是一种追求理想的热忱，与任何远赴异邦从事宣教工作，或将公义原则与仁爱精神融入永久法律和制度之人的热忱一样真实。它们也必将永远成为光辉灿烂的典范，表明一种不顾障碍、不畏危险的热忱能够成就何等事业”。↩︎

  212. 他们是修士。直到亚历山大三世 (Alexander III.) 时期，这个修会才有司铎；他授予该会接纳司铎和教士的权利。为了遵行唯独司铎有赦罪权的新惯例，司铎便成为必需。在军事修会创立后的第一个世纪，会众在公开的会务会议上认罪，并奉总团长之命，受公开鞭笞或其他惩罚。关于告解以及由司铎赦罪的严格教会法规，直到后来才由第四次拉特兰会议 (Fourth Lateran Council) 和托马斯·阿奎那 (Thomas Aquinas) 加以界定。见利 (Lea)，《圣殿骑士的赦罪程式》(The Absolution Formula of the Templars)。↩︎

  215. 关于欧洲各会院须供应的亚麻布及其他物品的数量，见乌尔霍恩 (Uhlhorn)。该修会有一个女性分支，但关于它的情况，所知甚少。1188年，阿拉贡王后桑查 (Sancha) 在萨拉戈萨 (Saragossa) 附近的锡赫纳 (Sixena)，为它建立了一所富裕的修院。↩︎

  216. 1898年10月31日，德意志皇帝威廉二世 (William II.) 在访问耶路撒冷期间，为新教的救赎主教堂举行献堂礼。这座教堂建于圣约翰骑士医院的旧址，与圣墓教堂相对。↩︎

  217. Templarii, fratres militiae templi, equites templarii, pauperes commilitiones Christi templique Salamonis（圣殿会众、圣殿军团弟兄、圣殿骑士、基督与所罗门圣殿的贫穷战友），都是他们曾使用的名称。医院骑士与圣殿骑士之间的相似程度，远不及圣殿骑士与条顿骑士之间。见柯曾 (Curzon)，第xi页。↩︎

  218. 提尔的威廉 (William of Tyre)。见赫费勒 (Hefele)，V. 401及下页。↩︎

  219. 《新骑士团赞》(De laude novae militiae)。↩︎

  220. 关于圣伯纳德 (St. Bernard) 对该修会的贡献，见莫里森 (Morison) 所著传记，第141页及后，以及斯托尔斯 (Storrs) 所著传记，第567–574页。↩︎

  221. 在英格兰，他们最初定居于霍尔本 (Holborn) 外的旧圣殿院，1185年从那里迁至泰晤士河畔的新圣殿院。圣殿教堂于1240年竣工。M. 帕里斯 (M. Paris) 记述了献堂礼以及医院骑士所备办的筵席。约在1150年，斯蒂芬 (Stephen) 及其王后将几处地方赠予圣殿骑士。伍德豪斯 (Woodhouse)，第260页，列出了英格兰的二十七所会院。↩︎

  222. 1244年条（An.）。↩︎

  223. 指十三世纪末。这是德尚比尔 (de Chambure) 的估计。绍特米勒 (Schottmüller) 将其估为40,000,000法郎。提尔的威廉 (William of Tyre)，XII. 7，称他们的财产“极其庞大”。他们的财富与贪婪已成为人所共知的口实。↩︎

  224. 丰克 (Funk) 称亚历山大的诏书为该修会的《大宪章》(Magna Charta)。见《韦策尔—韦尔特百科全书》(Wetzer-Welte)，XI. 1315。↩︎

  225. 此处涉及《彼得前书》5:8；见柯曾 (Curzon)，第58页。↩︎

  226. Non nobis, Domine, non nobis sed tuo nomini da gloriam.（耶和华啊，荣耀不要归与我们，不要归与我们，要归在你的名下。）↩︎

  227. 柯曾 (Curzon)，XXVII。↩︎

  228. Fugiat feminae oscula Christi militia（基督的军团当避开女子的亲吻），见曼西 (Mansi)，XXI. 72；亦见施尼勒 (Schnürer)，第153页。↩︎

  229. 施尼勒 (Schnürer)，《会规》(Rule) 第XI条，第138页。↩︎

  230. M. 帕里斯 (M. Paris)，卢阿德 (Luard) 编本，IV. 337及后，收录了1244年耶路撒冷牧首与圣殿骑士团副团长的书信。这位编年史家严厉谴责圣殿骑士的狂妄自大，以及他们出于嫉妒而与医院骑士相争。他们曾因医院骑士被安排居于首位，而拒绝随阿马尔里克王 (King Amalric) 前往埃及，这便是这种嫉妒的一个例子。↩︎

  231. 他们的堡垒中，有阿卡 (Acre) 附近建于1218年的朝圣者堡 (castle Pilgrim)；雅各·德维特里 (James de Vitry) 曾描述它的宏大与壮丽。↩︎

  232. 十三世纪，该修会的会院成为法国和英格兰重要的金融中心，为国王、主教和贵族提供安全保管款项、金银器皿、珠宝及重要文书的地方。亨利三世 (Henry III.) 及其他英格兰国王，和法国国王一样，都曾向他们借款。圣殿骑士也为意大利银行家贷出的款项代为支付，或担任其他款项的受托管理人；例如约翰 (John) 答应每年付给其嫂贝伦加丽娅 (Berengaria) 的一千马克。约翰经常在伦敦的圣殿骑士会院停留。见坎宁安 (Cunningham)，《英国工商业的发展》(Growth of English Industries and Commerce)，第3版；利奥波德·德利尔 (Leopold Delisle)，《圣殿骑士的金融业务》(Les operationsfinancières des Templiers)，巴黎，1889年；埃莉诺·费里斯 (Eleanor Ferris)，《圣殿骑士团与英格兰王室的财政关系》(Financial Relations of the Knights Templars to the English Crown)，载《美国历史评论》("Am. Hist. Rev.")，1902年10月。↩︎

  233. 沙拉松 (Charasson)，《讷伊的富尔克传》(Vie de Foulques de Neuilly)，第89页及下页，引霍夫登的理查德 (Richard de Hoveden)。↩︎

  234. Deutscher Orden, Ordo S. Mariae Theutonicorum.（德意志修会，条顿圣马利亚修会。）↩︎

  235. 名为 domus hospitalis S. Mariae Theutonicorum in Jerusalem（耶路撒冷条顿圣马利亚医院）。1128年，一所德意志医院在耶路撒冷奉献给圣马利亚。↩︎

  236. 1416年，在康斯坦茨会议 (Council of Constance) 上，波兰国王对他们以刀剑迫人归信的权利提出抗议。↩︎

  237. 在巴伐利亚的路易 (Lewis the Bavarian) 与教廷的冲突中，条顿骑士团支持皇帝，并从他那里获得了重大的赠与和特权。↩︎

  238. Fratres militiae Christi, gladiferi（基督军团弟兄，佩剑者），一个创立于1202年的军事修会。↩︎

  239. Kleider aus, Kleider an, Essen, Trinken, Schlafengehen, ist die Arbeit so die Deutsche Herren han.（脱衣，穿衣，吃饭，喝酒，上床睡觉，这就是条顿老爷们的工作。）↩︎

  240. 路德 (Luther) 于1523年写了一篇小册子，呼吁条顿骑士放弃虚假的独身会规，实行婚姻中真正的贞洁。《劝条顿修会诸君远避虚假的贞洁，持守真正的婚姻贞洁》(Ermahnung an die Herren Deutschen Ordens falsche Keuschheit zu meiden und zur rechten ehelichen Keuschheit zu greifen)。阿尔布雷希特 (Albrecht) 将路德宗宗教改革引入勃兰登堡 (Brandenburg)。他娶了丹麦公主多萝西娅 (Dorothea)。↩︎

  241. 西班牙和葡萄牙有几个兼立军事与宗教誓愿的修会，曾效力于对摩尔人 (Moors) 的战争。孔波斯特拉的圣雅各骑士团 (order of Iago of Campostella) 于1175年获得教皇认可，保护前往孔波斯特拉圣祠的朝圣者。卡拉特拉瓦骑士团 (order of Calatrava) 于1164年获得教皇批准，并积极参与对摩尔人的斗争。阿尔坎塔拉骑士团 (order of Alcantara) 于1183年获得卢修斯三世 (Lucius III.) 认可。在笃信天主教的斐迪南 (Ferdinand the Catholic) 统治下，后两个团体的领导权转归王室。↩︎
